**Ukraine**[^cite_note-10] is a country in [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe). It is the [second-largest country in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area) after [Russia](25391.md), which [borders it](36430484.md) to the east and northeast.[^cite_note-11] Ukraine also borders [Belarus](3457.md) to the north; [Poland](22936.md) and [Slovakia](26830.md) to the west; [Hungary](13275.md), [Romania](25445.md) and [Moldova](19260.md)[^cite_note-12] to the southwest; and the [Black Sea](3386.md) and the [Sea of Azov](78019.md) to the south and southeast.[^cite_note-13] [Kyiv](585629.md) is Ukraine's capital and [largest city](4077166.md), followed by [Kharkiv](2788558.md), [Odesa](55545.md), and [Dnipro](989795.md). The [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) is [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language). Ukraine covers about 600,000 km<sup>2</sup> (230,000 sq mi), with around [32 million people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Ukraine).&#x20;

Humans have inhabited Ukraine since at least 32,000 BC. During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), it was the site of [early Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Slavs) expansion and later became a key centre of [East Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavs) culture under the state of [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27), which emerged in the 9th century. Kievan Rus' became the largest and most powerful realm in Europe in the 10th and 11th centuries, but gradually disintegrated into rival regional powers before being destroyed by the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Kievan_Rus%27) in the 13th century. For the next 600 years the area was contested, divided, and ruled by a variety of external powers, including the [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania), the [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland), the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), the [Austrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire), the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), and the [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia).&#x20;

The [Cossack Hetmanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_Hetmanate) emerged in [central Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Ukraine) in the 17th century but was partitioned between Russia and Poland before being gradually absorbed by the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) in the 18th century. [Ukrainian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_nationalism) developed and, following the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) in 1917, the short-lived [Ukrainian People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_People%27s_Republic) was formed. The [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks) [consolidated control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian%E2%80%93Soviet_War) over much of the former empire and established the [Ukrainian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic), which became a [constituent republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union) of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) in 1922. In the early 1930s, millions of Ukrainians died in the [Holodomor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holodomor), a [human-made famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causes_of_the_Holodomor). During [World War II](32927.md), Ukraine was [occupied by Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Ukraine) and endured [major battles and atrocities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_in_Ukraine), resulting in 7 million civilians killed, including [most Ukrainian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_Ukraine).&#x20;

Ukraine gained independence in 1991 as the [Soviet Union dissolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), declaring itself [neutral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_country).[^cite_note-gska2.rada.gov.ua-14] [A new constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Ukraine) was adopted in 1996 as the country transitioned to a free market liberal democracy amid [endemic corruption](33085862.md#Political_corruption) and a legacy of state control.[^cite_note-15] The [Orange Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Revolution) of 2004–2005 ushered electoral and constitutional reforms. Resurgent political crises prompted a series of mass demonstrations in 2014 known as the [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan), leading to [a revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity), at the end of which Russia unilaterally [occupied](71944314.md) and [annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation) Ukraine's [Crimean Peninsula](163045.md), and [pro-Russian unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_pro-Russian_unrest_in_Ukraine) culminated in [a war in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)) with Russia and Russian-backed separatists. The [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) began a [new phase of the war](70149799.md).[^cite_note-\:5-16][^cite_note-\:6-17][^cite_note-European_Commission_Trade_Ukraine-18]&#x20;

Ukraine is a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) and [its system of government](1372585.md) is a [semi-presidential republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_republic). [Ukraine's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%27s_economy) is a [transition economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transition_economy), with the [lowest nominal GDP per capita in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_in_Europe_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita) as of 2024. Due to [its extensive fertile land](81265102.md), the country is an important exporter of [grain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain),[^cite_note-\:9-19] though grain production has [declined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Russian_theft_of_Ukrainian_grain) since 2022 due to the Russo-Ukrainian war, endangering global [food security](216361.md).[^cite_note-\:8-20][^cite_note-\:9-19] Ukraine is considered a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) in global affairs. [Its military](42799055.md) is the [sixth largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_military_and_paramilitary_personnel) with the [eighth largest defence budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_military_expenditures), and [operates](76045069.md) one of the world's largest and most diverse [drone fleets](337755.md). Ukraine is a founding member of the [United Nations](31769.md) and a member of the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe), the [World Trade Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization), and the [OSCE](22796.md). It is negotiating to [join the European Union](36830143.md) and applied to join [NATO](21133.md) in 2022.[^cite_note-21]&#x20;

## Name

Main article: [Name of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Ukraine)

"The Ukraine" redirects here. For the book, see [*The Ukraine* (book)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ukraine_\(book\)).

The name of Ukraine is frequently interpreted as coming from the [old Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Slavic) term for 'borderland' as is the word *[krajina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krajina)*.[^cite_note-22] Another interpretation is that the name of Ukraine means "region" or "country".&#x20;

In the [English-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-speaking_world) during most of the 20th century, Ukraine (whether independent or not) was referred to as "the Ukraine".[^cite_note-merriam-webster-23] This is because in Russian, the word *ukraina* means 'borderland'[^cite_note-24] so the [definite article](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_\(grammar\)) would be natural in the English language; this is similar to *_Nederlanden_*, which means 'low lands' and is rendered in English as "*the* [Netherlands](21148.md)".[^cite_note-25] However, since Ukraine's [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_of_Ukraine) in 1991, this usage has become politicised and more rare, and [style guides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Style_guide) advise against its use.[^cite_note-UKrW812991TU-26][^cite_note-27] U.S. ambassador [William Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_B._Taylor_Jr.) said that using "the Ukraine" implies disregard for Ukrainian sovereignty.[^cite_note-28] The official Ukrainian position is that "the Ukraine" is both grammatically and politically incorrect.[^cite_note-BBC_News_Magazine-29]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Ukraine)

### Early history



Early [Indo-European migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_migrations) from the [Pontic steppes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_steppes) of present-day Ukraine and Russia

[^cite_note-30]

Evidence for the earliest securely dated hominin presence in Europe comes from 1.4 million-year-old stone tools from [Korolevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korolevo), in western Ukraine.[^cite_note-Garba2024-31] Settlement by [modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_humans) in Ukraine and its vicinity dates back to 32,000 BC, with evidence of the [Gravettian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravettian_culture) in the [Crimean Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Mountains).[^cite_note-orig-32][^cite_note-bbc-33] By 4,500 BC, the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) [Cucuteni–Trypillia culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cucuteni%E2%80%93Trypillia_culture) was flourishing in wide areas of modern Ukraine, including [Trypillia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trypillia) and the entire [Dnieper](380138.md)-[Dniester](376633.md) region. Ukraine is a probable location for the first [domestication of the horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication_of_the_horse).[^cite_note-34] The [Kurgan hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurgan_hypothesis) places the Volga-Dnieper region of Ukraine and southern Russia as the [linguistic homeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic_homeland) of the [Proto-Indo-Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-Europeans).[^cite_note-35] Early [Indo-European migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_migrations) from the Pontic steppes in the 3rd millennium BC spread [Yamnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamnaya_culture) [Steppe pastoralist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Steppe_Herders) ancestry and [Indo-European languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages) across large parts of Europe.[^cite_note-36] During the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age), the land was inhabited by [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Iranian_languages)-speaking [Cimmerians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cimmerians), [Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythians), and [Sarmatians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarmatians).[^cite_note-37] Between 700 BC and 200 BC it was part of the [Scythian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythia) kingdom.[^cite_note-38]&#x20;

From the 6th century BC, [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome), and [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) colonies were established on the north-eastern shore of the [Black Sea](3386.md), such as at [Tyras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyras), [Olbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olbia,_Ukraine), and [Chersonesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chersonesus). These thrived into the 6th century AD. The [Goths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goths) stayed in the area, but came under the sway of the [Huns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huns) from the 370s. In the 7th century, the territory that is now eastern Ukraine was the centre of [Old Great Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Great_Bulgaria). At the end of the century, the majority of Bulgar tribes migrated in different directions, and the [Khazars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khazars) took over much of the land.[^cite_note-39]&#x20;

In the 5th and 6th centuries, the [Antes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antes_\(people\)), which some relate as an [early Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Slavs) people, lived in Ukraine. Migrations from the territories of present-day Ukraine throughout the [Balkans](4829.md) established many [South Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavs) nations. Northern migrations, reaching almost to [Lake Ilmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Ilmen), led to the emergence of the [Ilmen Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilmen_Slavs) and [Krivichs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krivichs). Following an [Avar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Avars) raid in 602 and the collapse of the Antes Union, most of these peoples survived as separate tribes until the beginning of the second millennium.[^cite_note-40]&#x20;

### Golden Age of Kyiv

Main articles: [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27), [Principality of Kiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Kiev), and [Kingdom of Galicia–Volhynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Galicia%E2%80%93Volhynia)



The furthest extent of [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27), 1054–1132

The establishment of the state of [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27) remains obscure and uncertain.[^cite_note-41] The state included much of present-day Ukraine, Belarus and the western part of [European Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Russia).[^cite_note-Columbia-42] According to the *[Primary Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_Chronicle)*, the [Rus' people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rus%27_people) initially consisted of [Varangians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varangian) from [Scandinavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavia).[^cite_note-43] In 882, the pagan [Prince Oleg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleg_of_Novgorod) (Oleh) conquered [Kyiv](585629.md) from [Askold and Dir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Askold_and_Dir) and proclaimed it as the new capital of the Rus'.[^cite_note-44] [Anti-Normanist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Normanism) historians however argue that the East Slavic tribes along the southern parts of the [Dnieper River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dnieper_River) were already in the process of forming a state independently.[^cite_note-martin-45] The Varangian elite, including the ruling [Rurik dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rurik_dynasty), later assimilated into the Slavic population.[^cite_note-Columbia-42] Kievan Rus' was composed of several [principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality) ruled by the interrelated Rurikid *[kniazes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knyaz)* ("princes"), who often fought each other for possession of Kyiv.[^cite_note-46]&#x20;

During the 10th and 11th centuries, Kievan Rus' became the largest and most powerful state in Europe, a period known as its Golden Age.[^cite_note-cia-47] It began with the reign of [Vladimir the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_the_Great) (980–1015), who [introduced Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus%27). During the reign of his son, [Yaroslav the Wise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaroslav_the_Wise) (1019–1054), Kievan Rus' reached the zenith of its cultural development and military power.[^cite_note-Columbia-42] The state soon fragmented as the relative importance of regional powers rose again. After a final resurgence under the rule of [Vladimir II Monomakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_II_Monomakh) (1113–1125) and his son [Mstislav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mstislav_I_of_Kiev) (1125–1132), Kievan Rus' finally disintegrated into separate principalities following Mstislav's death, though ownership of Kyiv would still carry great prestige for decades.[^cite_note-48] In the 11th and 12th centuries, the nomadic confederacy of the [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages)-speaking [Cumans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumans) and [Kipchaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kipchaks) was the dominant force in the [Pontic steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_steppe) north of the Black Sea.[^cite_note-49]&#x20;

The [Mongol invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Rus%27) in the mid-13th century devastated Kievan Rus'; following the [Siege of Kyiv in 1240](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Kyiv_\(1240\)), the city was destroyed by the Mongols.[^cite_note-50] In the western territories, the principalities of [Halych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Halych) and [Volhynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Volhynia) had arisen earlier, and were merged to form the [Principality of Galicia–Volhynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Galicia%E2%80%93Volhynia).[^cite_note-51] [Daniel of Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_of_Galicia), son of [Roman the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_the_Great), re-united much of south-western Rus', including [Volhynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volhynia), [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Eastern_Europe\)), as well as Kyiv. He was subsequently crowned by a [papal](23056.md) envoy as the first [king of Galicia–Volhynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Ruthenia) (also known as the Kingdom of [Ruthenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruthenia)) in 1253.[^cite_note-52]&#x20;

### Foreign domination

Further information: [Kiev Voivodeship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiev_Voivodeship)

See also: [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania), [Crown of the Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_the_Kingdom_of_Poland), [Crimean Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate), [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), and [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire)



The [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth) at its maximum extent in 1619, superimposed on modern borders. [Poland](22936.md) and the [Polish Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_the_Kingdom_of_Poland) exercised power over much of Ukraine after [1569](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Lublin). \
&#x20;  [Crown of the Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_the_Kingdom_of_Poland)\
&#x20;  [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania)\
&#x20;  [Duchy of Livonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Livonia)\
&#x20;  [Duchy of Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Prussia), Polish [fief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fief)\
&#x20;  [Duchy of Courland and Semigallia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Courland_and_Semigallia), Commonwealth fief&#x20;

In 1349, in the aftermath of the [Galicia–Volhynia Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia%E2%80%93Volhynia_Wars), the region was partitioned between the [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland) and the [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania).[^cite_note-rowell266-53] From the mid-13th century to the late 1400s, the [Republic of Genoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa) founded numerous [colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonies) on the northern coast of the Black Sea and transformed these into large commercial centres headed by the consul, a representative of the Republic.[^cite_note-54] In 1430, the region of [Podolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podolia) was incorporated into Poland, and the lands of modern-day Ukraine became increasingly settled by [Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_people).[^cite_note-55] In 1441, [Genghisid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghisid) prince [Haci I Giray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haci_I_Giray) founded the [Crimean Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate) on the [Crimean Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Peninsula) and the surrounding steppes;[^cite_note-56] the Khanate orchestrated [Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars) [slave raids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean%E2%80%93Nogai_slave_raids_in_Eastern_Europe). Over the next three centuries, the [Crimean slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_slave_trade) would enslave an estimated two million in the region.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]&#x20;

In 1569, the [Union of Lublin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Lublin) established the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), and most of the Ukrainian lands were transferred from Lithuania to the [Crown of the Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_the_Kingdom_of_Poland), becoming *[de jure](43594.md)* Polish territory. Under the pressures of [Polonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polonisation), many landed gentry of Ruthenia converted to [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism) and joined the circles of the [Polish nobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_nobility); others joined the newly created [Ruthenian Uniate Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruthenian_Uniate_Church).[^cite_note-59]&#x20;

### Cossack Hetmanate

Main articles: [Cossack Hetmanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_Hetmanate) and [Zaporozhian Sich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaporozhian_Sich)

Deprived of native protectors among the Ruthenian nobility, the peasants and townspeople began turning for protection to the emerging [Zaporozhian Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaporozhian_Cossacks). In the mid-17th century, a Cossack military quasi-state, the [Zaporozhian Host](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaporozhian_Host), was formed by [Dnieper Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dnieper_Cossacks) and Ruthenian peasants.[^cite_note-zaporizhia-60] Poland exercised little real control over this population, but found the Cossacks to be useful against the [Turks](22278.md) and Tatars,[^cite_note-britcos-61] and at times the two were allies in [military campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_wars_in_Europe).[^cite_note-62] However, the continued harsh [enserfment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serf) of Ruthenian peasantry by Polish [szlachta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Szlachta) (many of whom were Polonised [Ruthenian nobles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruthenian_nobility)) and the suppression of the Orthodox Church alienated the Cossacks.[^cite_note-britcos-61] The latter did not shy from taking up arms against those they perceived as enemies and occupiers, including the Catholic Church with its local representatives.[^cite_note-63]&#x20;



[Hetman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hetmans_of_Ukrainian_Cossacks) [Bohdan Khmelnytsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohdan_Khmelnytsky) established an independent [Cossack state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_Hetmanate) after the [1648 uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmelnytsky_Uprising) against Poland

In 1648, [Bohdan Khmelnytsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohdan_Khmelnytsky) led the [largest of the Cossack uprisings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmelnytsky_Uprising) against the Commonwealth and the [Polish king](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Polish_monarchs), which enjoyed wide support from the local population.[^cite_note-64] Khmelnytsky founded the [Cossack Hetmanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_Hetmanate), which existed until 1764 (some sources claim until 1782).[^cite_note-65] After Khmelnytsky suffered a crushing defeat at the [Battle of Berestechko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Berestechko) in 1651, he turned to the [Russian tsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_monarchs) for help. In 1654, Khmelnytsky was subject to the [Pereiaslav Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pereiaslav_Agreement), forming a military and political alliance with Russia that acknowledged loyalty to the Russian monarch.&#x20;

After his death, the Hetmanate went through a devastating 30-year war amongst Russia, Poland, the Crimean Khanate, the Ottoman Empire, and [Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossacks), known as "[The Ruin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ruin_\(Ukrainian_history\))" (1657–1686), for control of the Cossack Hetmanate. The [Treaty of Perpetual Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Perpetual_Peace_\(1686\)) between Russia and Poland in 1686 divided the lands of the Cossack Hetmanate between them, reducing the portion over which Poland had claimed sovereignty to Ukraine west of the Dnieper river. In 1686, the [Metropolitanate of Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitanate_of_Kyiv) was [annexed by the Moscow Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_the_Metropolitanate_of_Kyiv_by_the_Moscow_Patriarchate) through a synodal letter of the [Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople) [Dionysius IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysius_IV_of_Constantinople), thus placing the [Metropolitanate of Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitanate_of_Kyiv#Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine) under the authority of [Moscow](19004.md). An attempt to reverse the decline was undertaken by Cossack Hetman [Ivan Mazepa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Mazepa) (1639–1709), who ultimately defected to the [Swedes](5058739.md) in the [Great Northern War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Northern_War) (1700–1721) in a bid to get rid of Russian dependence,[^cite_note-Magocsi-66] but Hetmanate's capital city [Baturyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baturyn) was [sacked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sack_of_Baturyn) (1708) and they were crushed in the [Battle of Poltava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Poltava) (1709).[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-Magocsi-66]&#x20;

The Hetmanate's autonomy was severely restricted since Poltava. In the years 1764–1781, [Catherine the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_the_Great) incorporated much of [Central Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Ukraine) into the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), abolishing the Cossack Hetmanate and the [Zaporozhian Sich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaporozhian_Sich), and was one of the people responsible for the suppression of the last major Cossack uprising, the [Koliivshchyna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koliivshchyna).[^cite_note-68] After the [annexation of Crimea by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Empire) in 1783, the newly acquired lands, now called [Novorossiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novorossiya), were opened up to settlement by Russians.[^cite_note-69] The [tsarist autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsarist_autocracy) established a policy of [Russification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification), suppressing the use of the [Ukrainian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language) and curtailing the Ukrainian national identity.[^cite_note-censor-70] The western part of present-day Ukraine was subsequently split between Russia and [Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg)-ruled [Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire) after the [fall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland) of the Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth in 1795.&#x20;

### 19th and early 20th century

Main articles: [Southwestern Krai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Krai), [Kharkov Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharkov_Governorate), [Chernigov Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernigov_Governorate), [Ukrainian People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_People%27s_Republic), [Ukrainian State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_State), and [Ukrainian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic)

Further information: [Ukrainian national revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_national_revival), [Ukraine during World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_during_World_War_I), [Ukraine after the Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_after_the_Russian_Revolution), [Ukrainian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_War_of_Independence), and [Ukrainian–Soviet War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian%E2%80%93Soviet_War)



[Polish troops enter Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiev_offensive_\(1920\)) in May 1920 during the [Polish–Soviet War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Soviet_War). Following the [Peace of Riga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Riga) signed on 18 March 1921, Poland took control of modern-day western Ukraine while Soviets took control of eastern and central Ukraine

The 19th century saw the rise of Ukrainian nationalism. With growing urbanisation and modernisation and a cultural trend toward [romantic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_nationalism), a Ukrainian [intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligentsia) committed to national rebirth and social justice emerged. The serf-turned-national-poet [Taras Shevchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taras_Shevchenko) (1814–1861) and political theorist [Mykhailo Drahomanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mykhailo_Drahomanov) (1841–1895) led the growing nationalist movement.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] While conditions for its development in Austrian [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(eastern_Europe\)) under the [Habsburgs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburgs) were relatively lenient,[^cite_note-73] the Russian part (historically known as "[Little Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Russia)" or "South Russia")[^cite_note-74] faced severe restrictions, going as far as [banning virtually all books from being published in Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ems_Ukaz) in 1876.&#x20;

Ukraine, like the rest of the Russian Empire, joined the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) [later](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization_in_the_Russian_Empire) than most of Western Europe[^cite_note-75] due to the maintenance of [serfdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serfdom_in_Russia) until 1861. Other than near the newly discovered coal fields of the [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas), and in some larger cities such as [Odesa](55545.md) and Kyiv, Ukraine largely remained an agricultural and resource extraction economy.[^cite_note-76] The Austrian part of Ukraine [was particularly destitute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Austrian_Galicia), which forced hundreds of thousands of peasants into emigration, who created the backbone of an extensive [Ukrainian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_diaspora) in countries such as [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Canadians), the [United States](5938693.md) and [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Brazilians).[^cite_note-77] Some of the Ukrainians settled in the Far East, too. According to the [1897 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1897_census), there were 223,000 ethnic Ukrainians in [Siberia](29639.md) and 102,000 in [Central Asia](6742.md).[^cite_note-78] An additional 1.6 million emigrated to the east in the ten years after the opening of the [Trans-Siberian Railway](56912.md) in 1906.[^cite_note-79] [Far Eastern](173135.md) areas with an ethnic Ukrainian population became known as [Green Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Ukraine).[^cite_note-80]&#x20;

Ukraine plunged into turmoil with the beginning of [World War I](4764461.md), and fighting on Ukrainian soil persisted until late 1921. Initially, the Ukrainians were split between Austria-Hungary, fighting for the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers), though the vast majority served in the [Imperial Russian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Imperial_Russia), which was part of the [Triple Entente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Entente), under Russia.[^cite_note-81] As the Russian Empire collapsed, the conflict evolved into the [Ukrainian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_War_of_Independence), with Ukrainians fighting alongside, or against, the [Red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army), [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Army), [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makhnovshchina) and [Green armies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_armies), with the Poles, Hungarians (in [Transcarpathia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcarpathian_Rus%27)), and Germans also intervening at various times.&#x20;



Youth in national Ukrainian dress during a ceremony commemorating the 22 January 1919 "Act of Reunification of the Ukrainian People's Republic and the Western Ukrainian People's Republic", which is honoured yearly across 22 cities of Ukraine

An attempt to create an independent state, the left-leaning [Ukrainian People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_People%27s_Republic) (UPR), was first announced by [Mykhailo Hrushevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mykhailo_Hrushevsky), but the period was plagued by an extremely unstable political and military environment. It was first deposed in a [coup d'état](25914154.md) led by [Pavlo Skoropadskyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlo_Skoropadskyi), which yielded the [Ukrainian State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_State) under the German protectorate, and the attempt to restore the UPR under the [Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate_of_Ukraine) ultimately failed as the Ukrainian army was regularly overrun by other forces. The short-lived [West Ukrainian People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Ukrainian_People%27s_Republic) and [Hutsul Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutsul_Republic) also failed to join the rest of Ukraine.[^cite_note-82]&#x20;

The result of the conflict was a partial victory for the [Second Polish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic), which annexed the Western Ukrainian provinces, as well as a larger-scale victory for the pro-Soviet forces, which succeeded in dislodging the remaining factions and eventually established the [Ukrainian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) (Soviet Ukraine). Meanwhile, modern-day [Bukovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukovina) was occupied by [Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania) and [Carpathian Ruthenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Ruthenia) was admitted to [Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Czechoslovak_Republic) as an autonomous region.[^cite_note-83]&#x20;

The conflict over Ukraine, a part of the broader [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War), devastated the whole of the former Russian Empire, including eastern and central Ukraine. The fighting left over 1.5 million people dead and hundreds of thousands homeless in the former Russian Empire's territory. [Famine in 1921](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_famine_of_1921%E2%80%931922) further hit the eastern provinces.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85]&#x20;

### Inter-war period

See also: [Holodomor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holodomor)



Starved peasants on a street in [Kharkiv](2788558.md), 1933. [Collectivisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivisation) of crops and their confiscation by Soviet authorities led to a major famine in Soviet Ukraine known as the [Holodomor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holodomor)



[Les Kurbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Kurbas), one of the lead figures of the [Executed Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executed_Renaissance), was executed by the Soviet authorities, as many other Ukrainian intellectuals

[^cite_note-86]

[^cite_note-Kravchenko-87]

During the inter-war period, in Poland, Marshal [Józef Piłsudski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Pi%C5%82sudski) sought Ukrainian support by offering local autonomy as a way to minimise Soviet influence in Poland's eastern [Kresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kresy) region.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] However, this approach was abandoned after Piłsudski's death in 1935, due to continued unrest among the Ukrainian population, including assassinations of Polish government officials by the [Organisation of Ukrainian Nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_Ukrainian_Nationalists) (OUN); with the Polish government responding by restricting rights of people who declared Ukrainian nationality.[^cite_note-auto2-90][^cite_note-auto3-91] In consequence, the underground [Ukrainian nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_nationalism) and militant movement, which arose in the 1920s gained wider support.&#x20;

Meanwhile, the recently constituted Soviet Ukraine became one of the founding republics of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). During the 1920s,[^cite_note-92] under the Ukrainisation policy pursued by the national Communist leadership of [Mykola Skrypnyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mykola_Skrypnyk), Soviet leadership at first encouraged a national renaissance in [Ukrainian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Ukraine) and language. [Ukrainisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainisation) was part of the Soviet-wide policy of [Korenisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korenisation) (literally *indigenisation*), which was intended to promote the advancement of native peoples, their language and culture into the governance of their respective republics.&#x20;

Around the same time, Soviet leader [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin) instituted the [New Economic Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Economic_Policy) (NEP), which introduced a form of [market socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_socialism), allowing some private ownership of small and medium-sized productive enterprises, hoping to reconstruct the post-war Soviet Union that had been devastated by both WWI and later the civil war. The NEP was successful at restoring the formerly war-torn nation to pre-WWI levels of production and agricultural output by the mid-1920s, much of the latter based in Ukraine.[^cite_note-Service-93] These policies attracted many prominent former UNR figures, including former UNR leader Hrushevsky, to return to Soviet Ukraine, where they were accepted, and participated in the advancement of Ukrainian science and culture.[^cite_note-94] In July 1922, arrests and [deportations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union) of Ukrainian intellectuals (e.g. university professors) began in Soviet Ukraine and continued throughout the 1920s.[^cite_note-95]&#x20;

This period was cut short when [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) became the leader of the USSR following Lenin's death. Stalin did away with the NEP in what became known as the [Great Break](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Break_\(USSR\)). Starting from the late 1920s and now with a [centrally planned economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy), Soviet Ukraine took part in an [industrialisation scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization_in_the_USSR) which quadrupled its industrial output during the 1930s.&#x20;

Furthermore, Stalin sought to suppress Ukraine's aspirations for independence by taking severe measures to eliminate the Ukrainian peasantry and eradicate the nation's intellectual and cultural elite.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-Kravchenko-87] As a consequence of Stalin's new policy, the Ukrainian peasantry suffered from the [programme of collectivisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivization_in_the_USSR) of agricultural crops. Collectivisation was part of the [first five-year plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_five-year_plan_\(Soviet_Union\)) and was enforced by regular troops and the secret police known as [Cheka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheka). Those who resisted were [arrested and deported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union) to [gulags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag) and work camps. As members of the collective farms were sometimes not allowed to receive any grain until unrealistic quotas were met, millions starved to death in a [famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Droughts_and_famines_in_Russia_and_the_Soviet_Union) known as the [Holodomor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holodomor) or the "Great Famine", which was recognised by some countries as an act of [genocide](12441.md) perpetrated by Joseph Stalin and other Soviet notables.[^cite_note-97]&#x20;

Following on the Russian Civil War and collectivisation, the [Great Purge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Purge), while killing Stalin's perceived political enemies, resulted in a profound loss of a new generation of Ukrainian intelligentsia, known today as the [Executed Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executed_Renaissance).[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101]&#x20;

### World War II

See also: [Eastern Front (World War II)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)), [Reichskommissariat Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Ukraine), and [The Holocaust in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_Ukraine)

Following the [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland) in September 1939, [German](21212.md) and [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Soviet_Socialist_Republics) troops divided the territory of Poland. Thus, Eastern Galicia and Volhynia with their Ukrainian population became part of Ukraine. For the first time in history, the nation was united.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] Further territorial gains were secured in 1940, when the Ukrainian SSR incorporated the northern and southern districts of [Bessarabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabia), [Northern Bukovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Bukovina), and the [Hertsa region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hertsa_region) from the territories the USSR [forced Romania to cede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_Bessarabia_and_Northern_Bukovina), though it handed over the western part of the [Moldavian Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavian_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) to the newly created [Moldavian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavian_SSR). These territorial gains of the USSR were internationally recognised by the [Paris peace treaties of 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Treaties,_1947).[^cite_note-104]&#x20;

[German armies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht) [invaded the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa) on 22 June 1941, initiating nearly four years of [total war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_war). The [Axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_Powers) initially advanced against desperate but unsuccessful efforts of the Red Army. In the [battle of Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kyiv_\(1941\)), the city was acclaimed as a "[Hero City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hero_City_\(Soviet_Union\))", because of its fierce resistance. More than 600,000 Soviet soldiers (or one-quarter of the [Soviet Western Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Western_Front)) were killed or taken captive there, with many suffering [severe mistreatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_mistreatment_of_Soviet_prisoners_of_war).[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] After its conquest, most of the Ukrainian SSR was organised within the [Reichskommissariat Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Ukraine), with the intention of exploiting its resources and eventual German settlement. Some western Ukrainians, who had only joined the Soviet Union in 1939, hailed the Germans as liberators, but that did not last long as the Nazis made little attempt to exploit dissatisfaction with Stalinist policies.[^cite_note-ww2-107] Instead, the Nazis preserved the collective-farm system, carried out [genocidal policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_graves_in_the_Soviet_Union) against [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Ukraine), [deported millions of people to work in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OST-Arbeiter), and began a depopulation programme to prepare for German colonisation.[^cite_note-ww2-107] They blockaded the transport of food on the Dnieper River.[^cite_note-108]&#x20;

Although the majority of Ukrainians fought in or alongside the Red Army and [Soviet resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_partisans),[^cite_note-worldwars-109] in Western Ukraine an independent [Ukrainian Insurgent Army](1045217.md) movement arose (UPA, 1942). It was created as the armed forces of the underground [Organisation of Ukrainian Nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Ukrainian_Nationalists) (OUN).[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-vedeneyev-111] Both organisations, the OUN and the UPA, supported the goal of an [independent Ukrainian state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Ukrainian_Independence,_1941) on the territory with a Ukrainian ethnic majority. Although this brought conflict with Nazi Germany, at times the [Melnyk wing of the OUN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melnykites) allied with the Nazi forces. From mid-1943 until the end of the war, the UPA carried out [massacres of ethnic Poles](464698.md) in the Volhynia and [Eastern Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Galicia) regions, killing around 100,000 Polish civilians, which brought reprisals.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-IPNconf-113] These organised massacres were an attempt by the OUN to create a homogeneous Ukrainian state without a Polish minority living within its borders, and to prevent the post-war Polish state from asserting its sovereignty over areas that had been part of pre-war Poland.[^cite_note-114] After the war, the UPA continued to fight the USSR until the 1950s.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] At the same time, the [Ukrainian Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Liberation_Army), another nationalist movement, fought alongside the Nazis.[^cite_note-Kalb2015-117]&#x20;



[Kyiv](585629.md) suffered significant damage during [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)), and was occupied by the [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht) from 19 September 1941 until 6 November 1943

In total, the number of ethnic Ukrainians who fought in the ranks of the Soviet Army is estimated from 4.5 million[^cite_note-worldwars-109] to 7 million;[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-fn1-119] half of the [Pro-Soviet partisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Partisans) guerrilla resistance units, which counted up to 500,000 troops in 1944, were also Ukrainian.[^cite_note-120] Generally, the Ukrainian Insurgent Army's figures are unreliable, with figures ranging anywhere from 15,000 to as many as 100,000 fighters.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]&#x20;

The vast majority of the fighting in World War II took place on the [Eastern Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)).[^cite_note-123] The [total losses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties_of_the_Soviet_Union) inflicted upon the Ukrainian population during the war are estimated at 6 million,[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-dt-kul-dem-los-125] including an estimated one and a half million Jews killed by the [Einsatzgruppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einsatzgruppen),[^cite_note-126] sometimes with the help of local collaborators. Of the estimated 8.6 million Soviet troop losses,[^cite_note-peremoga7-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-Krivosheev-129] 1.4 million were ethnic [Ukrainians](145144.md).[^cite_note-peremoga7-127][^cite_note-Krivosheev-129][^cite_note-fn1-119][^cite_note-130] The [Victory Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_Day_over_Nazism_in_World_War_II) is celebrated as one of eleven Ukrainian national holidays.[^cite_note-131]&#x20;

### Post–war Soviet Ukraine

Further information: [Anti-Soviet resistance by the Ukrainian Insurgent Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Soviet_resistance_by_the_Ukrainian_Insurgent_Army) and [Chernobyl disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_disaster)

See also: [History of the Soviet Union (1953–1964)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Soviet_Union_\(1953%E2%80%931964\)), [History of the Soviet Union (1964–1982)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Soviet_Union_\(1964%E2%80%931982\)), and [History of the Soviet Union (1982–1991)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Soviet_Union_\(1982%E2%80%931991\))



Two future leaders of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) (left, pre-war [CPSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) chief in Ukraine) and [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev) (an engineer from [Kamianske](1261408.md), Ukraine)

The republic was heavily damaged by the war, and it required significant efforts to recover. More than 700 cities and towns and 28,000 villages were destroyed.[^cite_note-132] The situation was worsened by a [famine](51329.md) in 1946–1947, which was caused by a drought and the wartime destruction of infrastructure, killing at least tens of thousands of people.[^cite_note-dt-kul-dem-los-125] In 1945, the Ukrainian SSR became one of the founding members of the [United Nations](31769.md) (UN),[^cite_note-un_ukssr-133] part of a special agreement at the [Yalta Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_Conference), and, alongside Belarus, had voting rights in the UN even though they were not independent.[^cite_note-134] Moreover, Ukraine once more expanded its borders as it annexed [Zakarpattia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakarpattia_Oblast), and the population became much more homogenised due to post-war population transfers, most of which, as in the case of [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_and_expulsion_of_Germans_\(1944%E2%80%931950\)) and [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_the_Crimean_Tatars), were forced. As of 1 January 1953, Ukrainians were second only to Russians among adult "[special deportees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_settlements_in_the_Soviet_Union)", comprising 20% of the total.[^cite_note-Malynovska-135]&#x20;

Following the death of Stalin in 1953, [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) became the new leader of the USSR, who began the policies of [de-stalinisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Stalinization) and the [Khrushchev Thaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khrushchev_Thaw). During his term as head of the Soviet Union, [Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Oblast) was [transferred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_transfer_of_Crimea) from the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_SFSR) to the [Ukrainian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_SSR), formally as a friendship gift to Ukraine and for economic reasons.[^cite_note-136] This represented the final extension of Ukrainian territory and formed the basis for the internationally recognised borders of Ukraine to this day. Many top positions in the Soviet Union were occupied by Ukrainians, including notably [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev), [General Secretary of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) from 1964 to 1982. However, it was he and his [appointee in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Ukraine_\(Soviet_Union\)), [Volodymyr Shcherbytsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volodymyr_Shcherbytsky), who presided over the extensive [Russification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russification_of_Ukraine) of Ukraine and who were instrumental in repressing a new generation of Ukrainian intellectuals known as the [Sixtiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixtiers).[^cite_note-137]&#x20;

By 1950, the republic had fully surpassed pre-war levels of industry and production.[^cite_note-138] Soviet Ukraine soon became a European leader in industrial production[^cite_note-139] and an important centre of the Soviet [arms industry](608296.md) and high-tech research, though heavy industry still had an outsided influence.[^cite_note-140] The Soviet government invested in hydroelectric and nuclear power projects to cater to the energy demand that the development carried. On 26 April 1986, however, a reactor in the [Chernobyl Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_Nuclear_Power_Plant) exploded, resulting in the [Chernobyl disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_disaster), the worst [nuclear reactor](22151.md) accident in history.[^cite_note-141]&#x20;

### Independence

Further information: [Modern history of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_history_of_Ukraine), [Dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), [Orange Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Revolution), [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity), and [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md)



Ukrainian president [Leonid Kravchuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Kravchuk) and Russian president [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) signing the [Belavezha Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belavezha_Accords), which [dissolved the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), on 8 December 1991

[Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) pursued a policy of limited liberalisation of public life, known as *[perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika),* and attempted to reform a [stagnating economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Stagnation). The latter failed, but the democratisation of the Soviet Union fuelled nationalist and separatist tendencies among the ethnic minorities, including Ukrainians.[^cite_note-142] As part of the so-called [parade of sovereignties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parade_of_sovereignties), on 16 July 1990, the newly elected [Supreme Soviet of the Ukrainian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Soviet_of_the_Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) adopted the [Declaration of State Sovereignty of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_State_Sovereignty_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-gska2.rada.gov.ua-14] After a [failed coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat_attempt) by some Communist leaders in Moscow at deposing Gorbachov, outright independence was proclaimed on 24 August 1991.[^cite_note-143] It was approved by 92% of the Ukrainian electorate in a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Ukrainian_independence_referendum) on 1 December.[^cite_note-Nohlen_Stöver-144] Ukraine's new [president](20688479.md), Leonid Kravchuk, went on to sign the [Belavezha Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belavezha_Accords) and made Ukraine a founding member of the much looser [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md) (CIS),[^cite_note-145] though Ukraine never became a full member of the latter as it did not ratify the agreement founding CIS.[^cite_note-\:2-146] These documents sealed the fate of the Soviet Union, which formally voted itself out of existence on 26 December.[^cite_note-147]&#x20;

Ukraine was initially viewed as having favourable economic conditions in comparison to the other regions of the Soviet Union,[^cite_note-148] though it was one of the poorer Soviet republics by the time of the dissolution.[^cite_note-Notstronk-149] However, during its transition to the market economy, the country experienced deeper economic slowdown than almost all of the other [former Soviet Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Former_Soviet_Republics). During the recession, between 1991 and 1999, Ukraine lost 60% of its GDP[^cite_note-IMF-150][^cite_note-151] and suffered from [hyperinflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation) that peaked at 10,000% in 1993.[^cite_note-152] The situation only stabilised well after the new currency, the [hryvnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hryvnia), fell sharply in late 1998 partially as a fallout from the [Russian debt default](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_debt_default) earlier that year.[^cite_note-153] The legacy of the economic policies of the nineties was the mass privatisation of state property that created a class of extremely powerful and rich individuals known as the [oligarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_oligarch).[^cite_note-Notstronk-149] The country then fell into a series of sharp recessions as a result of the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession),[^cite_note-Notstronk-149] the start of the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md) in 2014,[^cite_note-154] and finally, the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) by Russia on 24 February 2022.[^cite_note-155] Ukraine's economy in general underperformed since the time independence came due to pervasive [corruption](33085862.md) and mismanagement,[^cite_note-156] which, particularly in the 1990s, led to protests and organised strikes.[^cite_note-157] The war with Russia impeded meaningful economic recovery in the 2010s,[^cite_note-158] while efforts to combat the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Ukraine), which arrived in 2020, were made much harder by [low vaccination rates](63319438.md)[^cite_note-159] and, later in the pandemic, by the ongoing invasion.[^cite_note-160]&#x20;



[Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) protest in Kyiv, December 2013

From the political perspective, one of the defining features of the [politics of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Ukraine) is that for most of the time, it has been divided along two issues: the relation between Ukraine, the [West](21208200.md) and Russia, and the classical [left-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left%E2%80%93right_political_spectrum) divide.[^cite_note-161] The first two presidents, Kravchuk and [Leonid Kuchma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Kuchma), tended to balance the competing visions of Ukraine,[^cite_note-162] though [Yushchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yushchenko) and [Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanukovych) were generally pro-Western and pro-Russian, respectively. There were two major protests against Yanukovych: the [Orange Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Revolution) in 2004, when tens of thousands of people went in protest of [election rigging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_rigging) in his favour (Yushchenko was eventually elected president), and another one in the winter of 2013/2014, when more gathered on the [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) to oppose Yanukovych's refusal to sign the [European Union–Ukraine Association Agreement](39410190.md). By the end of the protests on 21 February 2014, he fled from Ukraine and was removed by the parliament in what is termed the [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity), but Russia refused to recognise the interim pro-Western government, calling it a *[junta](216815.md)* and denouncing the events as a coup d'état sponsored by the United States.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-Partido_da_imprensa_Golpista-165]&#x20;

Despite the signing of the [Budapest memorandum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest_Memorandum_on_Security_Assurances) in 1994, in which Ukraine agreed to hand over [nuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) in exchange for guarantees of security and territorial integrity, Russia reacted violently to these developments and [started a war](42085878.md) against its western neighbour. In late February and early March 2014, it [annexed Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation) using its [Navy](383258.md) in [Sevastopol](59166849.md) as well as the so- called [little green men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_\(Russo-Ukrainian_War\)); after this succeeded, it then launched a [proxy war in the Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)) via the breakaway [Donetsk People's Republic](42425747.md) and [Luhansk People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhansk_People%27s_Republic).[^cite_note-166] The first months of the conflict with the Russian-backed separatists were fluid, but Russian forces then started an open invasion in Donbas on 24 August 2014. Together they pushed back Ukrainian troops to the frontline established in February 2015, i.e. after Ukrainian troops [withdrew from Debaltseve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Debaltseve).[^cite_note-167] It remained a [frozen conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frozen_conflict) until the early hours of 24 February 2022,[^cite_note-168] when Russia launched the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md).[^cite_note-169] A year later, Russian troops controlled about 17% of Ukraine's internationally recognised territory, which constitutes 94% of [Luhansk Oblast](494904.md), 73% of [Kherson Oblast](1273370.md), 72% of [Zaporizhzhia Oblast](494936.md), 54% of [Donetsk Oblast](494889.md) and all of Crimea,[^cite_note-170] though Russia failed with its initial plan, with Ukrainian troops recapturing some territory in counteroffensives.[^cite_note-171]&#x20;



[Russian-occupied territories of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine) as of 28 February 2026

The military conflict with Russia shifted the government's policy towards the West. Shortly after Yanukovych fled Ukraine, the country signed the EU association agreement in June 2014, and its citizens were granted visa-free travel to the European Union three years later. In January 2019, the [Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine) was recognised as independent of Moscow, which reversed the 1686 decision of the patriarch of Constantinople and dealt a further blow to Moscow's influence in Ukraine.[^cite_note-172] Finally, amid a full-scale war with Russia, Ukraine was granted [candidate status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potential_enlargement_of_the_European_Union) to the European Union on 23 June 2022.[^cite_note-BBC_News-173] A broad anti-corruption drive began in early 2023 with the resignations of several deputy ministers and regional heads during a reshuffle of the government.[^cite_note-174]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Ukraine)



Topographic map of Ukraine with borders and cities

Ukraine is the [second-largest European country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area), after Russia, and the largest country entirely in Europe. Lying between latitudes [44°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/44th_parallel_north) and [53° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/53rd_parallel_north), and longitudes [22°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/22nd_meridian_east) and [41° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/41st_meridian_east)., it is mostly in the [East European Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_European_Plain). Ukraine covers an area of 603,550 square kilometres (233,030 sq mi), with a coastline of 2,782 kilometres (1,729 mi).[^cite_note-cia-47]&#x20;

The landscape of Ukraine consists mostly of fertile [steppes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppes) (plains with few trees) and plateaus, crossed by rivers such as the Dnieper, [Seversky Donets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seversky_Donets), Dniester and the [Southern Bug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Bug) as they flow south into the Black Sea and the smaller [Sea of Azov](78019.md). To the southwest, the [Danube Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danube_Delta) forms the border with Romania. Ukraine's regions have diverse geographic features, ranging from the highlands to the lowlands. The country's only mountains are the [Carpathian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains) in the west, of which the highest is [Hoverla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoverla) at 2,061 metres (6,762 ft), and the Crimean Mountains, in the extreme south along the coast.[^cite_note-175]&#x20;

Ukraine also has a number of highland regions such as the [Volyn-Podillia Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volhynian-Podolian_Upland) (in the west) and the Near-Dnipro Upland (on the right bank of the Dnieper). To the east there are the south-western spurs of the [Central Russian Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Russian_Upland) over which runs the border with Russia. Near the Sea of Azov are the Donets Ridge and the Near Azov Upland. The [snow melt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_melt) from the mountains feeds the rivers and their [waterfalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterfalls_of_Ukraine).&#x20;

Significant natural resources in Ukraine include [lithium](17561.md),[^cite_note-176] natural gas,[^cite_note-\:0-177] [kaolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaolin),[^cite_note-\:0-177] timber[^cite_note-178] and an abundance of [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land).[^cite_note-179] Ukraine has many environmental issues.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181] Some regions lack adequate supplies of potable water.[^cite_note-182] Air and water pollution affects the country, as well as deforestation, and radiation contamination in the northeast from the 1986 accident at the [Chernobyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl) Nuclear Power Plant.[^cite_note-183] The environmental damage caused by the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) has been described as an [ecocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecocide).[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-ObserverEcocide-185] Within its first year alone, the conflict inflicted an estimated \$51 billion in environmental damage, resulting in severe toxic soil pollution and millions of tonnes of contaminated debris.[^cite_note-YaleE360Cost-186] These ecological crises were further compounded in June 2023 by the catastrophic [destruction of the Kakhovka Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_the_Kakhovka_Dam).&#x20;

### Climate



[Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) map of Ukraine

Ukraine is in the mid-latitudes, and generally has a [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate), except for its southern coasts, which have [cold semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_semi-arid_climate) and [humid subtropical climates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate).[^cite_note-faoclimate-187] Average annual temperatures range from 5.5–7 °C (41.9–44.6 °F) in the north, to 11–13 °C (51.8–55.4 °F) in the south.[^cite_note-ebclimate-188] [Precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation_\(meteorology\)) is highest in the west and north and lowest in the east and southeast.[^cite_note-ebclimate-188] Western Ukraine, particularly in the Carpathian Mountains, receives around 120 centimetres (47.2 in) of precipitation annually, while Crimea and the coastal areas of the Black Sea receive around 40 centimetres (15.7 in).[^cite_note-ebclimate-188]&#x20;

Water availability from the major river basins is expected to decrease [due to climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change), especially in summer. This poses risks to the agricultural sector.[^cite_note-189] The negative [impacts of climate change on agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_agriculture) are mostly felt in the south of the country, which has a [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe) climate. In the north, some crops may be able to benefit from a longer growing season.[^cite_note-190] The [World Bank](36752.md) has stated that Ukraine is highly [vulnerable to climate change](66204363.md).[^cite_note-191]&#x20;

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Wildlife of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Ukraine)

See also: [List of ecoregions in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ecoregions_in_Ukraine)



View from the western slope of Mount Ai-Petri of the [Ai-Petri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ai-Petri) plateau, in Crimea designated by the Ukrainian government as a natural heritage site

Ukraine contains six terrestrial [ecoregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecoregion): [Central European mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_European_mixed_forests), [Crimean Submediterranean forest complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Submediterranean_forest_complex), [East European forest steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_European_forest_steppe), [Pannonian mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_mixed_forests), [Carpathian montane conifer forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_montane_conifer_forests), and Pontic steppe.[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-192] There is somewhat more [coniferous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conifer) than [deciduous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous) forest.[^cite_note-ShvidenkoBukshaKrakovska2017-193] The most densely forested area is [Polisia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polisia) in the northwest, with pine, oak, and birch.[^cite_note-ShvidenkoBukshaKrakovska2017-193] There are 45,000 species of animals (mostly invertebrates),[^cite_note-Conference2001-194] with approximately 385 endangered species listed in the [Red Data Book of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Data_Book_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-State-195] [Internationally important wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsar_site) cover over 7,000 square kilometres (2,700 sq mi), with the Danube Delta being important for conservation.[^cite_note-wetland-196][^cite_note-Ramsar-197]&#x20;

## Politics

Main articles: [Politics of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Ukraine) and [Elections in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Ukraine)

Ukraine is a republic under a [semi-presidential system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system) with separate [legislative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_branch), [executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_branch), and [judicial branches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_branch).[^cite_note-Choudhry_2018_p16.-198]&#x20;

### Constitution

Main article: [Constitution of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Ukraine)



Chart of the political system of Ukraine

The Constitution of Ukraine was adopted and ratified at the 5th session of the [Verkhovna Rada](844993.md), the parliament of Ukraine, on 28 June 1996.[^cite_note-UNIANCD28616-199] The constitution was passed with 315 ayes out of 450 votes possible (300 ayes minimum).[^cite_note-UNIANCD28616-199] All other laws and other normative legal acts of Ukraine must conform to the constitution. The right to amend the constitution through a special legislative procedure is vested exclusively in the parliament. The only body that may interpret the constitution and determine whether legislation conforms to it is the [Constitutional Court of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Ukraine). Since 1996, the [public holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holiday) [Constitution Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Day_\(Ukraine\)) is celebrated on 28 June.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-UW29121996-201] On 7 February 2019, the Verkhovna Rada voted to amend the constitution to state Ukraine's strategic objectives as joining the [European Union](9317.md) and [NATO](21133.md).[^cite_note-202]&#x20;

### Government

Main article: [Government of Ukraine](1372585.md)



[Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md)\
[President](20688479.md)&#x20;



[Serhii Koretskyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serhii_Koretskyi)\
[Prime Minister](1256678.md)&#x20;

The president is elected by popular vote for a five-year term and is the formal [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state).[^cite_note-203] Ukraine's legislative branch includes the 450-seat [unicameral](204304.md) parliament, the Verkhovna Rada.[^cite_note-204] The parliament is primarily responsible for the formation of the executive branch and the [Cabinet of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Ministers_of_Ukraine), headed by the [prime minister](1256678.md).[^cite_note-205] The president retains the authority to nominate the ministers of foreign affairs and of defence for parliamentary approval, as well as the power to appoint the [prosecutor general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecutor_General_of_Ukraine) and the head of the [Security Service](709222.md).[^cite_note-206]&#x20;

Laws, acts of the parliament and the cabinet, presidential decrees, and acts of the [Crimean parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verkhovna_Rada_of_Crimea) may be abrogated by the Constitutional Court, should they be found to violate the constitution. Other normative acts are subject to judicial review. The [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Ukraine) is the main body in the system of courts of general jurisdiction. Local self-government is officially guaranteed. Local councils and city mayors are popularly elected and exercise control over local budgets. The heads of regional and district administrations are appointed by the president in accordance with proposals of the prime minister.[^cite_note-House2004-207]&#x20;

The [V-Dem Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute) found ongoing [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) in Ukraine as of 2025[^cite_note-j496-208] due to restrictions on [freedom of speech](21401843.md) and [freedom of association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_association).[^cite_note-l511-209]&#x20;

### Courts and law enforcement

Main articles: [Judicial system of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_system_of_Ukraine) and [Law enforcement in Ukraine](11137543.md)



[Klovsky Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klov_Palace), seat of the [Supreme Court of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Ukraine)

[Martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Ukraine) was declared when Russia invaded in February 2022,[^cite_note-210] and continues.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212] The courts enjoy legal, financial and constitutional freedom guaranteed by Ukrainian law since 2002. Judges are largely well protected from dismissal (except for gross misconduct). Court justices are appointed by presidential decree for an initial period of five years, after which Ukraine's Supreme Council confirms their positions for life. Although there are still problems, the system is considered to have been much improved since Ukraine's independence in 1991. The Supreme Court is regarded as an independent and impartial body, and has on several occasions ruled against the Ukrainian government. The [World Justice Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Justice_Project) ranks Ukraine 66 out of 99 countries surveyed in its annual Rule of Law Index.[^cite_note-213]&#x20;

[Prosecutors](440936.md) in Ukraine have greater powers than in most European countries, and according to the [European Commission for Democracy through Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Commission_for_Democracy_through_Law) "the role and functions of the Prosecutor's Office is not in accordance with [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) standards".[^cite_note-214] The [conviction rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conviction_rate) is over 99%,[^cite_note-215] equal to the conviction rate of the Soviet Union, with suspects often being incarcerated for long periods before trial.[^cite_note-\:7-216]&#x20;



The [Cabinet of Ministers](1372585.md) [building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Building,_Kyiv)

In 2010, [President Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych) formed an expert group to make recommendations on how to "clean up the current mess and adopt a law on court organisation".[^cite_note-\:7-216] One day later, he stated "We can no longer disgrace our country with such a court system."[^cite_note-\:7-216] The criminal judicial system and the prison system of Ukraine remain quite punitive.[^cite_note-United_States_Department_of_State_2021-217]&#x20;

Since 2010 court proceedings can be held in Russian by mutual consent of the parties. Citizens unable to speak Ukrainian or Russian may use their native language or the services of a translator.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] Previously all court proceedings had to be held in Ukrainian.[^cite_note-United_States_Department_of_State_2021-217]&#x20;

Law enforcement agencies are controlled by the [Ministry of Internal Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Internal_Affairs_of_Ukraine). They consist primarily of the [national police force](44656231.md) and various specialised units and agencies such as the [State Border Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Border_Guard_Service_of_Ukraine) and the [Coast Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Sea_Guard) services. Law enforcement agencies, particularly the police, faced criticism for their heavy handling of the 2004 Orange Revolution. Many thousands of police officers were stationed throughout the capital, primarily to dissuade protesters from challenging the state's authority but also to provide a quick reaction force in case of need; most officers were armed.[^cite_note-NYTSBU-220]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Administrative divisions of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Ukraine), [Ukrainian historical regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_historical_regions), and [List of cities in Ukraine](4077166.md)

Further information: [Political status of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_status_of_Crimea) and [Russian-occupied territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories)



Ukraine (2021) — major cities and adjacent countries

The system of Ukrainian subdivisions reflects the country's status as a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) (as stated in the country's constitution) with unified legal and [administrative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government) regimes for each unit.&#x20;
