Including [Sevastopol](51587.md) and the [Autonomous Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Republic_of_Crimea) that were annexed by the Russian Federation in 2014, Ukraine consists of 27 regions: twenty-four [oblasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oblast) (provinces), one [autonomous republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_republic) ([Autonomous Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Republic_of_Crimea)), and two cities of special status—[Kyiv](585629.md), the capital, and [Sevastopol](51587.md). The 24 oblasts and Crimea are subdivided into 136 *[raions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raion)* (districts) and city municipalities of regional significance, or second-level administrative units.[^cite_note-221]&#x20;

[Populated places in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populated_places_in_Ukraine) are split into two categories: urban and rural. Urban populated places are split further into cities and [urban-type settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban-type_settlement) (a Soviet administrative invention), while rural populated places consist of villages and settlements (a generally used term). All cities have a certain degree of self-rule depending on their significance such as national significance (as in the case of Kyiv and Sevastopol), regional significance (within each oblast or autonomous republic) or district significance (all the rest of cities). A city's significance depends on several factors such as its population, socio-economic and historical importance and infrastructure.&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Ukraine)

See also: [International membership of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_membership_of_Ukraine), [Ukraine–European Union relations](5612265.md), [Accession of Ukraine to the European Union](36830143.md), [Ukraine and the World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_and_the_World_Bank), and [Ukrainian nationality law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_nationality_law)



[President of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Georgia) [Salome Zurabishvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Zourabichvili), [President of Moldova](1118398.md) [Maia Sandu](40796149.md), [Ukrainian President](20688479.md) [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) and [European Council President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_European_Council) [Charles Michel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Michel) during the 2021 International Conference in [Batumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batumi). In 2014, the EU signed association agreements with all three countries

From 1999 to 2001, Ukraine served as a non-permanent member of the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council). Historically, Soviet Ukraine joined the United Nations in 1945 as one of the original members following a Western compromise with the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-U.S._Department_of_State,_Bureau_of_Public_Affairs,_Office_of_Public_Communication,_Editorial_Division.2000-222] Ukraine has consistently supported peaceful, negotiated settlements to disputes. It has participated in the quadripartite talks on the conflict in Moldova and promoted a peaceful resolution to the conflict in the [post-Soviet state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states) of Georgia. Ukraine also has made contributions to UN [peacekeeping](84617.md) operations since 1992.[^cite_note-NATO_Information_Service.-223]&#x20;

Ukraine considers Euro-Atlantic integration its primary foreign policy objective,[^cite_note-result_of_Russia-224] but in practice it has always balanced its relationship with the European Union and the United States with strong ties to Russia. The European Union's [Partnership and Cooperation Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partnership_and_Cooperation_Agreement) (PCA) with Ukraine went into force in 1998. The European Union (EU) has encouraged Ukraine to implement the PCA fully before discussions begin on an association agreement, issued at the EU Summit in December 1999 in [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki), recognises Ukraine's long-term aspirations but does not discuss association.[^cite_note-result_of_Russia-224]&#x20;

In 1992, Ukraine joined the then-Conference on Security and Cooperation in Europe (now the [Organisation for Security and Cooperation in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_for_Security_and_Cooperation_in_Europe) (OSCE)), and also became a member of the [North Atlantic Cooperation Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Cooperation_Council). [Ukraine–NATO relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93NATO_relations) are close and the country has declared interest in eventual membership.[^cite_note-result_of_Russia-224]&#x20;

&#x20;Ukraine is the most active member of the [Partnership for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partnership_for_Peace) (PfP). All major political parties in Ukraine support full eventual integration into the European Union.[^cite_note-225] The Association Agreement between Ukraine and the European Union was signed in 2014.[^cite_note-226] Ukraine long had close ties with all its neighbours, but [Russia–Ukraine relations](17446303.md) rapidly deteriorated in 2014 due to the annexation of Crimea, energy dependence and payment disputes.



In January 2016, Ukraine joined   the [Deep and Comprehensive Free Trade Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_and_Comprehensive_Free_Trade_Area) with   the EU, established by the [Ukraine–European Union Association Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93European_Union_Association_Agreement), opening its path towards [European integration](683704.md)

The [Deep and Comprehensive Free Trade Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_and_Comprehensive_Free_Trade_Area) (DCFTA), which entered into force in January 2016 following the ratification of the [Ukraine–European Union Association Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93European_Union_Association_Agreement), formally integrates Ukraine into the [European Single Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Single_Market) and the [European Economic Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Area).[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228] Ukraine receives further support and assistance for its [EU-accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_enlargement_of_the_European_Union) aspirations from the International Visegrád Fund of the [Visegrád Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visegr%C3%A1d_Group) that consists of [Central European](5188.md) [EU members](10890716.md) the Czech Republic, Poland, Hungary and Slovakia.[^cite_note-229]&#x20;

In 2020, in [Lublin](74594.md), Lithuania, Poland and Ukraine created the [Lublin Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lublin_Triangle) initiative, which aims to create further cooperation between the three historical countries of the Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth and further Ukraine's integration and accession to the [EU](9317.md) and NATO.[^cite_note-230]&#x20;

In 2021, the [Association Trio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_Trio) was formed by signing a joint memorandum between the [Foreign Ministers of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_Georgia), [Moldova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_and_European_Integration_of_Moldova) and [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Ukraine\)). The Association Trio is a tripartite format for enhanced cooperation, coordination, and dialogue between the three countries (that have signed the Association Agreement with the EU) with the European Union on issues of common interest related to [European integration](683704.md), enhancing cooperation within the framework of the [Eastern Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Partnership), and committing to the prospect of joining the European Union.[^cite_note-231] As of 2021, Ukraine was preparing to formally apply for EU membership in 2024, in order to join the European Union in the 2030s,[^cite_note-\:1-232] however, with the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy requested that the country be admitted to the EU immediately.[^cite_note-auto1-233] Candidate status was granted in June 2022.[^cite_note-BBC_News-173] In recent years, Ukraine has dramatically strengthened its ties with the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-\:6-17][^cite_note-\:5-16]&#x20;

In June 2025, Ukraine legalised multiple citizenship.[^cite_note-U24MediaPassports-234][^cite_note-KyivIndPassports-235]&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [Armed Forces of Ukraine](42799055.md)



Ukrainian troops on the move during the [2022 Ukrainian eastern counteroffensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Ukrainian_eastern_counteroffensive)

After the dissolution of the Soviet Union, Ukraine inherited a 780,000-man military force on its territory, equipped with the third-largest [nuclear weapons arsenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_and_Ukraine) in the world.[^cite_note-milgov-236][^cite_note-237] In 1992, Ukraine signed the [Lisbon Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon_Protocol) in which the country agreed to give up all nuclear weapons to Russia for disposal and to join the [Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Non-Proliferation_Treaty) as a non-nuclear weapon state. By 1996 the country had become free of nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-milgov-236]&#x20;

Ukraine took consistent steps toward reduction of conventional weapons. It signed the [Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Conventional_Armed_Forces_in_Europe), which called for reduction of tanks, artillery, and armoured vehicles (army forces were reduced to 300,000). The country planned to convert the current [conscript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscript)-based military into a professional [volunteer military](9354649.md).[^cite_note-wbook06-238] Ukraine's current military consist of 196,600 active personnel and around 900,000 reservists.[^cite_note-239]&#x20;



American [M142 HIMARS](147011.md) rocket launchers in Ukrainian service, an example of [foreign military equipment received](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War) during the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md)

Ukraine played an increasing role in peacekeeping operations. In 2014, the Ukrainian frigate *Hetman Sagaidachniy* joined the European Union's counter-piracy [Operation Atalanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Atalanta) and was part of the EU Naval Force off the coast of [Somalia](27358.md) for two months.[^cite_note-240] Ukrainian troops were deployed in [Kosovo](17391.md) as part of the [Ukrainian-Polish Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish-Ukrainian_Peace_Force_Battalion).[^cite_note-241] In 2003–2005, a Ukrainian unit was deployed as part of the [multinational force in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_force_in_Iraq) under Polish command.[^cite_note-242] Military units of other states participated in multinational military exercises with Ukrainian forces in Ukraine regularly, including [U.S. military](32212.md) forces.[^cite_note-243]&#x20;

Following independence, Ukraine declared itself a neutral state.[^cite_note-gska2.rada.gov.ua-14] The country had a limited military partnership with Russian Federation and other CIS countries and has had a partnership with NATO since 1994. In the 2000s, the government was leaning towards NATO, and deeper cooperation with the alliance was set by the NATO-Ukraine Action Plan signed in 2002. It was later agreed that the question of joining NATO should be answered by a national referendum at some point in the future.[^cite_note-wbook06-238] Deposed President Viktor Yanukovych considered the then level of co-operation between Ukraine and NATO sufficient, and was against Ukraine joining NATO. During the [2008 Bucharest summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Bucharest_summit), NATO declared that Ukraine would eventually become a member of NATO when it meets the criteria for accession.&#x20;

As part of its modernisation after the beginning of the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md) in 2014, junior officers were allowed to take more initiative and a [territorial defence force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_defence_battalions_\(Ukraine\)) of volunteers was established.[^cite_note-244] Various defensive weapons including [drones](337755.md) were supplied by many countries, but not fighter jets.[^cite_note-245] During the first few weeks of the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) the military found it difficult to defend against shelling, missiles and high level bombing; but light infantry used shoulder-mounted weapons effectively to destroy tanks, armoured vehicles and low-flying aircraft.[^cite_note-246] In August 2023, the U.S. officials estimated that up to 70,000 Ukrainian soldiers were killed and 100,000 to 120,000 wounded during the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md).[^cite_note-247]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Ukraine](31829.md)



[Kyiv](585629.md), the [financial centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_centre) of Ukraine

In 2021, agriculture was the biggest sector of the economy. Ukraine is one of the world's [largest wheat exporters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_wheat_exports). It remains among the [poorest countries in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_in_Europe_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita) with the lowest [nominal GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita).[^cite_note-poor-248] Despite improvements, as in Moldova [corruption in Ukraine](33085862.md) remains an obstacle to [joining the EU](36830143.md); the country was rated 104th out of 181 in the [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) for 2025.[^cite_note-249] In 2021, Ukraine's [GDP](12594.md) per capita by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) was just over \$14,000.[^cite_note-250] Despite supplying [emergency financial support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War), the [IMF](15251.md) expected the economy to shrink considerably by 35% in 2022 due to the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md).[^cite_note-auto4-251] One 2022 estimate was that post-war reconstruction costs might reach half a trillion dollars.[^cite_note-252]&#x20;

In 2021, the average salary in Ukraine reached its highest level at almost [₴](722696.md)14,300 (US\$525) per month.[^cite_note-253] About 1% of Ukrainians lived [below the national poverty line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_by_country) in 2019.[^cite_note-254] Unemployment in Ukraine was 4.5% in 2019.[^cite_note-255] In 2019 5–15% of the Ukrainian population were categorised as middle class.[^cite_note-256] In 2020 Ukraine's [government debt](695460.md) was roughly 50% of its nominal GDP.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-\:4-258]&#x20;

In 2021 mineral commodities and light industry were important sectors.[^cite_note-\:4-258] Ukraine produces nearly all types of transportation vehicles and [spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Space_Agency_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] The [European Union](9317.md) is the country's main trade partner, and remittances from Ukrainians working abroad are important.[^cite_note-\:4-258]&#x20;

### Agriculture



Wheat crop in Spasiv village, Rivne Oblast, Ukraine.

Ukraine is among the world's top agricultural producers and exporters and is often described as the "bread basket of Europe". During the 2020/21 international wheat marketing season (July–June), it ranked as the sixth largest wheat exporter, accounting for nine percent of world wheat trade.[^cite_note-\:02-262] The country is also a major global exporter of [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), barley and rapeseed. In 2020/21, it accounted for 12 percent of global trade in [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize) and [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley) and for 14 percent of world [rapeseed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapeseed) exports. Its trade share is even greater in the sunflower oil sector, with the country accounting for about 50 percent of world exports in 2020/2021.[^cite_note-\:02-262]&#x20;

According to the [Food and Agriculture Organisation of the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization) (FAO), further to causing the loss of lives and increasing humanitarian needs, the likely disruptions caused by the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md) to Ukraine's grain and oilseed sectors, could jeopardise the food security of many countries, especially those that are highly dependent on Ukraine and Russia for their food and fertiliser imports.[^cite_note-263] Several of these countries fall into the [Least Developed Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_countries) (LDC) group, while many others belong to the group of [Low-Income Food-Deficit Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Low-Income_Food-Deficit_Countries\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (LIFDCs).[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] For example [Eritrea](17238590.md) sourced 47 percent of its wheat imports in 2021 from Ukraine. Overall, more than 30 nations depend on Ukraine and the Russian Federation for over 30 percent of their wheat import needs, many of them in North Africa and Western and Central Asia.[^cite_note-\:02-262]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Ukraine)



[Kamianets-Podilskyi Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamianets-Podilskyi_Castle), one of the [Seven Wonders of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_Ukraine)

Before the [Russo-Ukrainian war](42085878.md) the number of tourists visiting Ukraine was eighth in Europe, according to [UN Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Tourism) [rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_rankings).[^cite_note-266] Ukraine has numerous tourist attractions: mountain ranges suitable for [skiing](28478.md), hiking and fishing; the [Black Sea](3386.md) coastline as a popular summer destination; [nature reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_reserve) of different [ecosystems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem); and churches, [castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle) ruins and other architectural and park landmarks. [Kyiv](585629.md), [Lviv](49968.md), [Odesa](55545.md) and [Kamianets-Podilskyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamianets-Podilskyi) were Ukraine's principal tourist centres, each offering many historical landmarks and extensive [hospitality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitality) infrastructure. The [Seven Wonders of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_Ukraine) and [Seven Natural Wonders of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Natural_Wonders_of_Ukraine) are selections of the most important landmarks of Ukraine, chosen by Ukrainian experts and an Internet-based public vote. Tourism was the mainstay of Crimea's economy before a major fall in visitor numbers following the Russian annexation in 2014.[^cite_note-267]&#x20;

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Ukraine)

HRCS2 unit

[HRCS2 multiple unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HRCS2_multiple_unit). [Rail transport](3149341.md) is heavily utilised in Ukraine.

Many roads and bridges were destroyed, and international maritime travel was blocked by the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md).[^cite_note-auto4-251] Before that it was mainly through the [Port of Odesa](20974479.md), from where ferries sailed regularly to [Istanbul](3391396.md), [Varna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varna,_Bulgaria) and [Haifa](55606.md). The largest ferry company operating these routes was [Ukrferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UkrFerry).[^cite_note-268] There are over 1,600 km (1,000 mi) of [navigable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navigability) waterways on 7 rivers, mostly on the [Danube](21209639.md), [Dnieper](380138.md) and [Pripyat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pripyat_\(river\)). All Ukraine's rivers freeze over in winter, limiting navigation.[^cite_note-269]&#x20;

[Ukraine's rail network](6011024.md) connects all major urban areas, port facilities and [industrial centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing). The heaviest concentration of [railway track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_track) is the [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas) region.[^cite_note-270] Although [rail freight transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_freight_transport) fell in the 1990s, Ukraine is still one of the [world's highest rail users](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_rail_usage).[^cite_note-271] Ukraine also has multiple [urban rail systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_rail_transit), particularly three metro ([Dnipro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dnipro_Metro), [Kharkiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharkiv_Metro), and [Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv_Metro)),[^cite_note-272] two [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail) ([Kryvyi Rih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kryvyi_Rih_Metrotram) and [Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv_Light_Rail)),[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] two urban *[elektrychka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elektrychka)* (Kamianske–Dnipro–Synelnykove and [Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv_Urban_Electric_Train)),[^cite_note-275] two [cable railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_railway) ([Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv_Funicular) and [Odesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odesa_Funicular)),[^cite_note-276] and numerous [tramway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_town_tramway_systems_in_Ukraine) systems.[^cite_note-277]&#x20;

[Ukraine International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_International_Airlines), is the [flag carrier](465816.md) and the largest [airline](1942.md), with its head office in [Kyiv](585629.md)[^cite_note-278] and its main hub at Kyiv's [Boryspil International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boryspil_International_Airport). It operated domestic and international passenger flights and cargo services to Europe, the Middle East, the United States,[^cite_note-auto1-233] Canada,[^cite_note-279] and Asia.&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Ukraine](32021039.md)



Electricity production by source in Ukraine

Energy in Ukraine is mainly from [gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_in_Ukraine), [oil](77988708.md) and [nuclear power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Ukraine).[^cite_note-280] [The coal industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_in_Ukraine) has been disrupted by conflict.[^cite_note-281] Most gas and oil is imported, but since 2015 [energy policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy) has prioritised diversifying energy supply.[^cite_note-282]&#x20;

About half of [electricity generation](9540.md) is nuclear and a quarter coal.[^cite_note-\:0-177] The largest [nuclear power plant](207347.md) in Europe, the [Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant](1763005.md), is in Ukraine. [Fossil fuel subsidies](44334234.md) were US\$2.2 billion in 2019.[^cite_note-283] Until the 2010s all of Ukraine's nuclear fuel came from Russia, but now most does not.[^cite_note-284]&#x20;

Some energy infrastructure was destroyed in the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md).[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] The contract to transit [Russian gas](42827258.md) expires at the end of 2024.[^cite_note-287]&#x20;

In early 2022 Ukraine and [Moldova](26337757.md) decoupled their electricity grids from the [Integrated Power System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPS/UPS#IPS) of Russia and [Belarus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Belarus); and the [European Network of Transmission System Operators for Electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Network_of_Transmission_System_Operators_for_Electricity) synchronised them with [continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronous_grid_of_Continental_Europe).[^cite_note-cbsnews-ukraine-grid-288][^cite_note-289]&#x20;

### Information technology

Main articles: [Economy of Ukraine § Information technology](31829.md#Information_technology), and [Internet in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_in_Ukraine)

Key officials may use [Starlink](45111627.md) as backup.[^cite_note-\:3-290] The IT industry contributed almost 5 per cent to Ukraine's GDP in 2021[^cite_note-291] and in 2022 continued both inside and outside the country.[^cite_note-292]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Ukraine) and [Ukrainians](145144.md)

Before the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), the country had an estimated population of over 41 million people, and was the [eighth-most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_population) in Europe. It is a [heavily urbanised country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization_by_country), and its industrial regions in the east and southeast are the most densely populated—about 67% of its total population lives in urban areas.[^cite_note-293] At that time Ukraine had a [population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_population_density) of 69.5 inhabitants per square kilometre (180 inhabitants/mi<sup>2</sup>), and the overall [life expectancy in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) at birth was 73 years (68 years for males and 77.8 years for females).[^cite_note-294]&#x20;

Following the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), Ukraine's population hit a peak of roughly 52 million in 1993. However, due to its [death rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_rate) exceeding its [birth rate](515898.md), mass emigration, poor living conditions, and low-quality health care,[^cite_note-nw-20260217-295][^cite_note-ukrstat-population-296] the total population decreased by 6.6 million, or 12.8% from the same year to 2014.&#x20;

According to the [2001 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Census_\(2001\)), ethnic [Ukrainians](145144.md) made up roughly 78% of the population, while [Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Ukraine) were the largest minority, at some 17.3% of the population. Small minority populations included: [Belarusians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusians) (0.6%), [Moldovans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldovans) (0.5%), [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars) (0.5%), [Bulgarians](151876.md) (0.4%), [Hungarians](17106358.md) (0.3%), [Romanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanians) (0.3%), [Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_people) (0.3%), [Jews](25955086.md) (0.3%), [Armenians](387816.md) (0.2%), [Greeks](42056.md) (0.2%) and [Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars) (0.2%).[^cite_note-Ethnic_composition_of_the_population_of_Ukraine,_2001_Census-2] It was also estimated that there were about 10–40,000 [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans) in Ukraine, who lived mostly in the south of the country, belonging to the historical [Koryo-saram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koryo-saram) group,[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] as well as about 47,600 [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) (though the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) estimates a higher number of about 260,000).[^cite_note-299]&#x20;

Outside the former Soviet Union, the largest source of incoming immigrants in Ukraine's post-independence period was from four Asian countries, namely China, India, Pakistan and Afghanistan.[^cite_note-mp-300] In the late 2010s 1.4 million Ukrainians were [internally displaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_displaced) due to the [war in Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas_\(2014%E2%80%932022\)),[^cite_note-301] and in early 2022, over 4.1 million fled the country after the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), causing the [Ukrainian refugee crisis](70199806.md).[^cite_note-302] Most male Ukrainian nationals aged 18 to 60 were denied exit from Ukraine.[^cite_note-303] The Ukrainian government estimates that the population in the regions controlled by Ukraine was 25 to 27 million in 2024.[^cite_note-304]&#x20;

### Urban areas

Main article: [List of cities in Ukraine](4077166.md)

Ukraine has 457 cities, of which 176 are designated as oblast-class, 279 as smaller *raion*-class cities, and two as special legal status cities. There are also 886 urban-type settlements and 28,552 villages.[^cite_note-oblasts-305]&#x20;

### Language

Main article: [Languages of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Ukraine)

Further information: [Ukrainian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language) and [Russian language in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language_in_Ukraine)



During the post-WW2 period, the Russification of ethnic Ukrainians,

[^cite_note-307]

&#x20;as well as the mass immigration of ethnic Russians,

[^cite_note-308]

&#x20;significantly changed the ethnic and linguistic composition of Ukraine's population. The change was especially evident in Donbas, where Ukrainian speakers became a minority.

According to Ukraine's constitution, the [state language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) is [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language).[^cite_note-SerhyYUBoaMN-309] [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) is widely spoken in the country, especially in eastern and southern Ukraine.[^cite_note-SerhyYUBoaMN-309][^cite_note-310] Most native Ukrainian speakers know Russian as a second language.[^cite_note-SerhyYUBoaMN-309] Russian was the *de facto* dominant language of the Soviet Union but Ukrainian also held official status in the republic,[^cite_note-311] and in the schools of the [Ukrainian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_SSR), learning Ukrainian was mandatory.[^cite_note-SerhyYUBoaMN-309]&#x20;

Ukrainian is the primary language used in the vast majority of Ukraine. 67% of Ukrainians speak Ukrainian as their primary language, while 30% speak Russian as their primary language.[^cite_note-312] In eastern and southern Ukraine, Russian is the primary language in some cities, while Ukrainian is used in rural areas. [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language) is spoken in [Chernivtsi Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernivtsi_Oblast) and parts of [Odesa](61438266.md) and [Zakarpattia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakarpattia_Oblast) oblasts,[^cite_note-313] while [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language) is spoken in [Zakarpattia Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakarpattia_Oblast).[^cite_note-unian.info-314] There is no consensus among scholars whether [Rusyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rusyn_language), also spoken in Zakarpattia, is a distinct language or a dialect of Ukrainian.[^cite_note-315] The Ukrainian government does not recognise Rusyn and [Rusyns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rusyns) as a distinct language and people.[^cite_note-316]&#x20;

For a large part of the Soviet era, the number of Ukrainian speakers declined from generation to generation, and by the mid-1980s, the usage of the Ukrainian language in public life had decreased significantly.[^cite_note-Shamshur-317] Following independence, the government of Ukraine began restoring the use of the Ukrainian language in schools and government through a policy of [Ukrainisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainisation).[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]&#x20;

Effective in August 2012, [a new law on regional languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislation_on_languages_in_Ukraine) entitled any local language spoken by at least a 10 percent minority be declared official within that area.[^cite_note-320] Within weeks, Russian was declared a regional language of several southern and eastern [oblasts](994124.md) (provinces) and cities.[^cite_note-321] Russian could then be used in the administrative office work and documents of those places.[^cite_note-NewUklang2892012-322][^cite_note-323]&#x20;

In 2014, following the [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity), the [Ukrainian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Parliament) voted to repeal the [law on regional languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Principles_of_State_Language_Policy), making Ukrainian the sole state language at all levels; however, the repeal was not signed by acting [President Turchynov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleksandr_Turchynov) or by President Poroshenko.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] In 2019, the law allowing for official use of regional languages was found unconstitutional.[^cite_note-327] According to the Council of Europe, this act fails to achieve fair protection of the [linguistic rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic_rights) of [minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_language).[^cite_note-328] Presently, most foreign films and TV programmes, including Russian ones, are subtitled or dubbed in Ukrainian.[^cite_note-329] Ukraine's 2017 [education law](5231751.md) bars primary education in public schools in grade five and up in any language but Ukrainian.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331]&#x20;



Linguistic map of Ukraine showing most common native language by city, town, or village council, according to the [2001 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Census_\(2001\))

Since 2014, during the Russian-Ukrainian war, Russia has been pursuing a policy of forced Russification of [the territories of Ukraine it has occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine) — schools teach only in Russian, even in entirely Ukrainian-speaking settlements. Ukrainian school textbooks have been banned, and those who wish to study in Ukrainian are forced to do so in secret from the occupation authorities.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] Ukrainian-language literature is also being destroyed on a massive scale.[^cite_note-334] According to the report "Ukraine: Forced Russified Education Under Occupation" by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), the Russian occupation authorities and other high-ranking Russian officials have taken and continue to take measures to eradicate the Ukrainian language in the occupied territories.[^cite_note-335] [Ukrainian children forcibly deported to Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abductions_in_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war) are also subjected to Russification.[^cite_note-336]&#x20;

At the same time, Russian armed aggression provoked significant changes in the Ukrainian society's perception of the language issue: a significant part of the formerly Russian-speaking population switched to speaking Ukrainian,[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] and the popularity of the idea of granting Russian official status throughout all of Ukraine or certain regions of the country has reached its lowest level since polling began in 1997.[^cite_note-339]&#x20;

### Diaspora

Main article: [Ukrainian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_diaspora)

The Ukrainian [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora) comprises [Ukrainians](145144.md) and their descendants who live outside Ukraine around the world, especially those who maintain some kind of connection to the land of their ancestors and maintain their feeling of Ukrainian national identity within their own local community.[^cite_note-340] The Ukrainian diaspora is found throughout numerous regions worldwide including other [post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states) as well as in [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Ukrainian),[^cite_note-Cecco_2022_u131-341] and other countries such as [Poland](12190815.md),[^cite_note-342] [the United States](5938693.md),[^cite_note-343] the UK[^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345] and [Brazil](3383.md).[^cite_note-346]&#x20;

The [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md) has led to the [Ukrainian refugee crisis](70199806.md) in which millions of Ukrainian civilians moved to neighbouring countries. Most crossed into Poland, Slovakia, and the Czech Republic, and others proceeded to at least temporarily settle in Hungary, Moldova, Germany, Austria, Romania and other European countries.[^cite_note-347]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Ukraine)



The [Saint Sophia Cathedral in Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Sophia%27s_Cathedral,_Kyiv), a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md),

[^cite_note-348]

&#x20;is one of the main Christian cathedrals in Ukraine

Ukraine has the world's [second-largest Eastern Orthodox population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_by_country), after Russia.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350] A 2021 survey conducted by the [Kyiv International Institute of Sociology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv_International_Institute_of_Sociology) (KIIS) found that 82% of Ukrainians declared themselves to be religious, while 7% were [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheists), and a further 11% found it difficult to answer the question.[^cite_note-351] The level of religiosity in Ukraine was reported to be the highest in [Western Ukraine](3258511.md) (91%), and the lowest in the [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas) (57%) and [Eastern Ukraine](13065940.md) (56%).[^cite_note-Razumkov2016Page27-352]&#x20;

In 2019, 82% of Ukrainians were Christians; out of which 72.7% declared themselves to be [Eastern Orthodox](10186.md), 8.8% [Ukrainian Greek Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Greek_Catholic_Church), 2.3% [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) and 0.9% [Latin Church Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church). Other [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian) comprised 2.3%. [Judaism](15624.md), [Islam](6037917.md), and [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) were the religions of 0.2% of the population each. According to the KIIS study, roughly 58.3% of the Ukrainian Orthodox population were members of the [Orthodox Church of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Ukraine), and 25.4% were members of the [Ukrainian Orthodox Church (Moscow Patriarchate)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Church_\(Moscow_Patriarchate\)).[^cite_note-353] [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants_in_Ukraine) are a growing community in Ukraine, who made up 1.9% of the population in 2016,[^cite_note-Razumkov2016Page29-354] but rose to 2.2% of the population in 2018.&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Ukraine)

See also: [Healthcare in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Ukraine)



Central Municipal Hospital in Kyiv



Ukrainian president Zelenskyy visiting a hospital in the [Odesa Oblast](61438266.md), where injured Ukrainian defenders are seen treated in July 2022 during the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md)

Ukraine's healthcare system is state subsidised and freely available to all Ukrainian citizens and registered residents. However, it is not compulsory to be treated in a state-run hospital as a number of private medical complexes do exist nationwide.[^cite_note-355] The public sector employs most healthcare professionals, with those working for private medical centres typically also retaining their state employment as they are mandated to provide care at public health facilities on a regular basis.[^cite_note-356]&#x20;

All of Ukraine's medical service providers and hospitals are subordinate to the [Ministry of Healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Healthcare_\(Ukraine\)), which provides oversight and scrutiny of general medical practice as well as being responsible for the day-to-day administration of the healthcare system. Despite this, standards of hygiene and patient-care have fallen.[^cite_note-357]&#x20;

Ukraine faces a number of major public health issues and is considered to be in a demographic crisis because of its high death rate, low birth rate, and high emigration.[^cite_note-358] A factor contributing to the high death rate is a high [mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate) among working-age males from preventable causes such as [alcohol poisoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_poisoning) and smoking.[^cite_note-worldbank1-359]&#x20;

Active reformation of Ukraine's healthcare system was initiated right after the appointment of [Ulana Suprun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulana_Suprun) as a head of the [Ministry of Healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Healthcare_\(Ukraine\)).[^cite_note-Rada_Reform-360] Assisted by deputy Pavlo Kovtoniuk, Suprun first changed the distribution of finances in healthcare.[^cite_note-Kovtoniuk-361] Funds must follow the patient. General practitioners will provide basic care for patients. The patient will have the right to choose one. Emergency medical service is considered to be fully funded by the state. [Emergency Medicine Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Medicine_Reform_in_Ukraine_since_2016) is also an important part of the healthcare reform. In addition, patients who suffer from chronic diseases, which cause a high toll of disability and mortality, are provided with free or low-price medicine.[^cite_note-Drugs-362]&#x20;

As a result of the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md), millions in Ukraine suffered physical injuries and psychological traumas.[^cite_note-363] The [World Health Organization](33583.md) has documented over 2,254 attacks on healthcare in Ukraine since the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md).[^cite_note-WHO-364] According to the October 2024 data of the World Health Organization Ukraine health needs assessment, 68% of Ukrainians reported that their health declined compared to the pre-war period.[^cite_note-WHO-364] The war with Russia worsened Ukrainian children physical and mental health.[^cite_note-365]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Ukraine](5231751.md)



The [University of Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taras_Shevchenko_National_University_of_Kyiv) is one of Ukraine's most important educational institutions.



The [Residence of Bukovinian and Dalmatian Metropolitans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residence_of_Bukovinian_and_Dalmatian_Metropolitans) by [Josef Hlávka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Hl%C3%A1vka), 1882, now [Chernivtsi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernivtsi_University)

According to the [Ukrainian constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Ukraine), access to free education is granted to all citizens. Complete general secondary education is compulsory in the state schools which constitute the overwhelming majority. Free higher education in state and communal educational establishments is provided on a competitive basis.[^cite_note-366]&#x20;

Because of the Soviet Union's emphasis on total access of education for all citizens, which continues today, the [literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_rate) is an estimated 99.4%.[^cite_note-cia-47] Since 2005, an eleven-year school programme has been replaced with a twelve-year one: primary education takes four years to complete (starting at age six), middle education (secondary) takes five years to complete; upper secondary then takes three years.[^cite_note-367] Students in the 12th grade take Government tests, which are also referred to as school-leaving exams. These tests are later used for university admissions.&#x20;

Among the oldest is also the [Lviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lviv_University), founded in 1661. More higher education institutions were set up in the 19th century, beginning with universities in [Kharkiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharkiv_University) (1805), [Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taras_Shevchenko_National_University_of_Kyiv) (1834), [Odesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odesa_University) (1865) and [Chernivtsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernivtsi_University) (1875) and a number of professional higher education institutions, e.g.: [Nizhyn Historical and Philological Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizhyn_Pedagogical_University) (originally established as the Gymnasium of Higher Sciences in 1805), a Veterinary Institute (1873) and a [Technological Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharkiv_Polytechnical_Institute) (1885) in [Kharkiv](2788558.md), a [Polytechnic Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Sikorsky_Kyiv_Polytechnic_Institute) in Kyiv (1898) and a Higher Mining School (1899) in [Katerynoslav](989795.md). Rapid growth followed in the [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) period. By 1988 the number of higher education institutions increased to 146 with over 850,000 students.[^cite_note-368]&#x20;

The Ukrainian higher education system comprises higher educational establishments, [scientific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific) and [methodological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodological) facilities under national, [municipal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_government) and self-governing bodies in charge of education.[^cite_note-369] The organisation of higher education in Ukraine is built up in accordance with the structure of education of the world's higher [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_countries), as is defined by [UNESCO](21786641.md) and the UN.[^cite_note-370]&#x20;

Ukraine produces the fourth largest number of [post-secondary graduates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education) in Europe, while being ranked seventh in population.[^cite_note-371] [Higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Ukraine) is either state funded or private. Most universities provide subsidised housing for out-of-city students. It is common for libraries to supply required books for all registered students. Ukrainian universities confer two degrees: the bachelor's degree (4 years) and the master's degree (5–6th year), in accordance with the [Bologna process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bologna_process). Historically, [Specialist degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specialist_degree) (usually 5 years) is still also granted; it was the only degree awarded by universities in Soviet times.[^cite_note-372] Ukraine was ranked 66th in 2025 in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index).[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374]&#x20;

### Regional differences

See also: [Demographics of Ukraine § Regional differences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Ukraine#Regional_differences), [Central Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Ukraine), [Eastern Ukraine](13065940.md), [Southern Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ukraine), and [Western Ukraine](3258511.md)



The results of the [2014 parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Ukrainian_parliamentary_election) with [People's Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Front_\(Ukraine\)) in yellow, [Opposition Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_Bloc) in blue and [Petro Poroshenko Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petro_Poroshenko_Bloc) in red

[Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language) is the dominant language in [Western Ukraine](3258511.md) and in [Central Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Ukraine), while [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) is the dominant language in the cities of [Eastern Ukraine](13065940.md) and [Southern Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ukraine). In the [Ukrainian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_SSR) schools, learning [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) was mandatory; in modern Ukraine, schools with Ukrainian as the language of instruction offer classes in Russian and in the other minority languages.[^cite_note-SerhyYUBoaMN-309][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-RatingJuly12-376][^cite_note-377]&#x20;

On the [Russian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language_in_Ukraine), on [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and [Ukrainian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_nationalism), opinion in Eastern Ukraine and Southern Ukraine tends to be the exact opposite of those in Western Ukraine; while opinions in Central Ukraine on these topics tend be less extreme.[^cite_note-RatingJuly12-376][^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379][^cite_note-KIISS1313-380]&#x20;

Similar historical divisions also remain evident at the level of individual social identification. Attitudes toward the most important political issue, relations with Russia, differed strongly between [Lviv](49968.md), identifying more with [Ukrainian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_nationalism) and the [Ukrainian Greek Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Greek_Catholic_Church), and [Donetsk](562951.md), predominantly Russian orientated and favourable to the [Soviet era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_era), while in central and southern Ukraine, as well as [Kyiv](585629.md), such divisions were less important and there was less antipathy toward people from other regions.[^cite_note-antipathy-381]&#x20;

However, all were united by an overarching Ukrainian identity based on shared economic difficulties, showing that other attitudes are determined more by culture and politics than by demographic differences.[^cite_note-antipathy-381][^cite_note-382] Surveys of regional identities in Ukraine have shown that the feeling of belonging to a "Soviet identity" is strongest in the [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas) (about 40%) and the Crimea (about 30%).[^cite_note-383]&#x20;

During [elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Ukraine) voters of Western and Central Ukrainian [oblasts](994124.md) (provinces) vote mostly for parties ([Our Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Ukraine%E2%80%93People%27s_Self-Defense_Bloc), [Batkivshchyna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Ukrainian_Union_%22Fatherland%22))[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385] and presidential candidates ([Viktor Yuschenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yuschenko), [Yulia Tymoshenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yulia_Tymoshenko)) with a [pro-Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-Western) and state reform [platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_platform), while voters in Southern and Eastern oblasts vote for parties ([CPU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Ukraine), [Party of Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Regions)) and presidential candidates ([Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych)) with a [pro-Russian](2541825.md) and [status quo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_quo) platform.[^cite_note-EWparties-386][^cite_note-Umland-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] However, this geographical division is decreasing.[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Ukrainian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_culture)



A collection of traditional Ukrainian Easter eggs—[pysanky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg_decorating_in_Slavic_culture#Pysanky). The design motifs on pysanky date back to early Slavic cultures



[Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_in_Ukraine) celebration in [Lviv](49968.md)

Ukrainian customs are heavily influenced by [Orthodox Christianity](10186.md), the dominant religion in the country.[^cite_note-393] Gender roles also tend to be more traditional, and grandparents play a greater role in bringing up children, than in the West.[^cite_note-394] The culture of Ukraine has also been influenced by its eastern and western neighbours, reflected in its [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_architecture), music and art.[^cite_note-395]&#x20;

The Communist era had quite a strong effect on the art and writing of Ukraine.[^cite_note-396] In 1932, Stalin made [socialist realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_realism) state policy in the Soviet Union when he promulgated the decree "On the Reconstruction of Literary and Art Organisations". This greatly stifled creativity. During the 1980s [glasnost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost) (openness) was introduced and Soviet artists and writers again became free to express themselves as they wanted.[^cite_note-397]&#x20;

As of 2023, UNESCO inscribed [8 properties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Ukraine) in Ukraine on the [World Heritage List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_List). Ukraine is also known for its decorative and folk traditions such as [Petrykivka painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrykivka_painting), [Kosiv ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosiv_painted_ceramics), and [Cossack songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_songs).[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401] Between February 2022 and March 2023, UNESCO verified the damage to 247 sites, including 107 [religious sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_site), 89 buildings of artistic or historical interest, 19 monuments and 12 libraries.[^cite_note-402] Since January 2023, the [historic centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Centre_of_Odesa) of [Odesa](55545.md) has been inscribed on the [List of World Heritage in Danger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_in_Danger).[^cite_note-403]&#x20;

The tradition of the [Easter eggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_egg), known as [pysanky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg_decorating_in_Slavic_culture#Pysanky), has long roots in Ukraine. These eggs were drawn on with wax to create a pattern; then, the dye was applied to give the eggs their pleasant colours, the dye did not affect the previously wax-coated parts of the egg. After the entire egg was dyed, the wax was removed leaving only the colourful pattern. This tradition is thousands of years old, and precedes the arrival of Christianity to Ukraine.[^cite_note-404] In the city of [Kolomyia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolomyia) near the foothills of the [Carpathian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains), the [museum of Pysanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pysanka_Museum) was built in 2000 and won a nomination as the monument of modern Ukraine in 2007, part of the [Seven Wonders of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_Ukraine) action.&#x20;

Since 2012, the Ministry of Culture of Ukraine has formed the [National Register of Elements of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_elements_in_Ukraine#National_Register),[^cite_note-405] which consists of 115 items as of September 2025.[^cite_note-National_Register-406]&#x20;

### Architecture

Main article: [Ukrainian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_architecture)



[St Michael's Golden-Domed Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Michael%27s_Golden-Domed_Monastery) in [Kyiv](585629.md), the foremost example of [Ukrainian Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Baroque) and one of Ukraine's most recognisable landmarks

Ukrainian architecture includes the motifs and styles that are found in structures built in modern Ukraine, and by [Ukrainians](145144.md) worldwide. These include [initial roots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Kievan_Rus%27) which were established in the state of [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27). Following the [Christianisation of Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus%27), Ukrainian architecture has been influenced by [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture). After the [Mongol invasion of Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Kievan_Rus%27), the [Galician style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_school_\(architecture\)) continued to develop in the [Kingdom of Galicia–Volhynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Galicia%E2%80%93Volhynia).[^cite_note-KatchanovskiKohutNebesio2013-407]&#x20;

After the union with the [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia), architecture in Ukraine began to develop in different directions, with many structures in the larger eastern, Russian-ruled area built in the styles of [Russian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_architecture) of that period, whilst the western region of [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Central_Europe\)) developed under [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Poland) and [Austro-Hungarian architectural influences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Austria).[^cite_note-KatchanovskiKohutNebesio2013-407] Still, a separate [Ukrainian Baroque style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Baroque) was developed by the [Ukrainian Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Cossacks) in 17th–18th centuries,[^cite_note-\:42-408] and [Ukrainian Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Art_Nouveau) had limited success in the 20th century.[^cite_note-409] Ukrainian national motifs would eventually be used during the period of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and in modern independent Ukraine.[^cite_note-KatchanovskiKohutNebesio2013-407] However, much of the contemporary architectural skyline of Ukraine is dominated by Soviet-style [Khrushchyovkas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khrushchyovka), or low-cost apartment buildings.[^cite_note-410]&#x20;

### Libraries

See also: [List of libraries in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_libraries_in_Ukraine)

The [Vernadsky National Library of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernadsky_National_Library_of_Ukraine), is the main academic library and main scientific information centre in Ukraine.&#x20;

During the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md), the Russians bombed the Maksymovych Scientific Library of the Taras Shevchenko Kyiv National University, [Vernadsky National Library of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernadsky_National_Library_of_Ukraine), the National Scientific Medical Library of Ukraine and the Kyiv City Youth Library.[^cite_note-411]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Ukrainian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_literature)

Ukrainian literature has origins in [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic) writings, which was used as a [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy) and [literary language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_language) following [Christianisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus%27) in the 10th and 11th centuries.[^cite_note-ualit-412][^cite_note-ualitmsn-413][^cite_note-414] Other writings from the time include [chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicle), the most significant of which was the *[Primary Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_Chronicle)*. Literary activity faced a sudden decline after the [Mongol invasion of Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Kievan_Rus%27), before seeing a revival beginning in the 14th century, and was advanced in the 16th century with the invention of the [printing press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_press).[^cite_note-ualit-412]&#x20;



[Taras Shevchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taras_Shevchenko)



[Lesya Ukrainka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesya_Ukrainka), one of the foremost Ukrainian women writers

The [Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossacks) established an independent society and popularised a [new kind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duma_\(epic\)) of [epic poem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_poetry), which marked a high point of Ukrainian [oral literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_literature).[^cite_note-ualitmsn-413] These advances were then set back in the 17th and early 18th centuries, as many Ukrainian authors wrote in Russian or Polish. Nonetheless, by the late 18th century, the modern literary Ukrainian language finally emerged.[^cite_note-ualit-412] In 1798, the modern era of the Ukrainian literary tradition began with [Ivan Kotliarevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Kotliarevsky)'s publication of [Eneida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eneida) in the Ukrainian vernacular.[^cite_note-415]&#x20;

By the 1830s, a Ukrainian [romantic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) began to develop, and the nation's most renowned cultural figure, romanticist poet-painter [Taras Shevchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taras_Shevchenko) emerged. Whereas Ivan Kotliarevsky is considered to be the father of literature in the Ukrainian vernacular; Shevchenko is the father of a national revival.[^cite_note-416]&#x20;

Then, in 1863, the use of the Ukrainian language in print was effectively [prohibited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ems_Ukaz) by the Russian Empire.[^cite_note-censor-70] This severely curtailed literary activity in the area, and Ukrainian writers were forced to either publish their works in Russian or release them in Austrian controlled [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Central_Europe\)). The ban was never officially lifted, but it became obsolete after the revolution and the Bolsheviks' coming to power.[^cite_note-ualitmsn-413]&#x20;

Ukrainian literature continued to flourish in the early Soviet years when nearly all literary trends were approved. These policies faced a steep decline in the 1930s, when prominent representatives as well as many others were killed by the [NKVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD) during the [Great Purge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Purge). In general around 223 writers were repressed by what was known as the [Executed Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executed_Renaissance).[^cite_note-417] These repressions were part of Stalin's implemented policy of [socialist realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_realism). The doctrine did not necessarily repress the use of the Ukrainian language, but it required that writers follow a certain style in their works.&#x20;

Literary freedom grew in the late 1980s and early 1990s alongside the decline and collapse of the USSR and the reestablishment of Ukrainian independence in 1991.[^cite_note-ualit-412]&#x20;

### Weaving and embroidery



[Rushnyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rushnyk), [Ukrainian embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_embroidery)

Artisan [textile arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_arts) play an important role in Ukrainian culture,[^cite_note-418] especially in [Ukrainian wedding traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_wedding_traditions). [Ukrainian embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_embroidery), [weaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving) and lace-making are used in traditional [folk dress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_dress) and in traditional celebrations. Ukrainian embroidery varies depending on the region of origin[^cite_note-419] and the designs have a long history of motifs, compositions, choice of colours and types of stitches.[^cite_note-museum-420] Use of colour is very important and has roots in [Ukrainian folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_folklore). Embroidery motifs found in different parts of Ukraine are preserved in the [Rushnyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rushnyk) Museum in [Pereiaslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pereiaslav).&#x20;

National dress is woven and highly decorated. Weaving with handmade looms is still practised in the village of Krupove, situated in [Rivne Oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rivne_Oblast). The village is the birthplace of two internationally recognised personalities in the scene of national crafts fabrication: Nina Myhailivna[^cite_note-421] and Uliana Petrivna.[^cite_note-422]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Ukraine)



[Cossack Mamay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_Mamay) playing a [kobza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kobza)



[Mykola Lysenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mykola_Lysenko) is widely considered to be the father of Ukrainian classical music

[^cite_note-Risch_2011_p._44-423]

Music is a major part of Ukrainian culture, with a long history and many influences. From traditional [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music), to [classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_music) and [modern rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_rock), Ukraine has produced several internationally recognised musicians including [Kirill Karabits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirill_Karabits), [Okean Elzy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okean_Elzy) and [Ruslana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruslana). Elements from traditional Ukrainian folk music made their way into Western music and even into modern [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz). Ukrainian music sometimes presents a perplexing mix of exotic melismatic singing with chordal harmony. The most striking general characteristic of authentic ethnic Ukrainian folk music is the wide use of minor modes or keys which incorporate augmented second intervals.[^cite_note-Sonevytsky_2019_p.-424]&#x20;

During the Baroque period, music had a place of considerable importance in the curriculum of the [Kyiv-Mohyla Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv-Mohyla_Academy). Much of the nobility was well versed in music with many Ukrainian Cossack leaders such as ([Mazepa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Mazepa), [Paliy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semen_Paliy), [Holovatyj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antin_Holovaty), [Sirko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Sirko)) being accomplished players of the [kobza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kobza), [bandura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandura) or [torban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torban).&#x20;

The first dedicated musical academy was set up in [Hlukhiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hlukhiv) in 1738 and students were taught to sing and play violin and bandura from manuscripts. As a result, many of the earliest composers and performers within the Russian empire were ethnically Ukrainian, having been born or educated in Hlukhiv or having been closely associated with this music school.[^cite_note-Struk_1993_p._1461-425] Ukrainian classical music differs considerably depending on whether the composer was of Ukrainian ethnicity living in Ukraine, a composer of non-Ukrainian ethnicity who was a citizen of Ukraine, or part of the [Ukrainian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_diaspora).[^cite_note-Ukrainian_people_2017-426]&#x20;

Since the mid-1960s, Western-influenced pop music has been growing in popularity in Ukraine. Folk singer and harmonium player [Mariana Sadovska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariana_Sadovska) is prominent. Ukrainian pop and folk music arose with the international popularity of groups and performers like [Vopli Vidoplyasova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vopli_Vidoplyasova), [Dakh Daughters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakh_Daughters), [Dakha Brakha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakha_Brakha), [Ivan Dorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Dorn) and [Okean Elzy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okean_Elzy).&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Media of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Ukraine)

The Ukrainian legal framework on media freedom is deemed "among the most progressive in eastern Europe", although implementation has been uneven.[^cite_note-FH-427] The constitution and laws provide for [freedom of speech](21401843.md)[^cite_note-428] and [press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press_in_Ukraine). The main regulatory authority for the broadcast media is the [National Television and Radio Broadcasting Council of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Television_and_Radio_Broadcasting_Council_of_Ukraine) (NTRBCU), tasked with licencing media outlets and ensure their compliance with the law.[^cite_note-429]&#x20;

[Kyiv](585629.md) dominates the media sector in Ukraine: National [newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Ukraine) *[Den](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Den_\(newspaper\))*, *[Dzerkalo Tyzhnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzerkalo_Tyzhnia)*, tabloids, such as *[The Ukrainian Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ukrainian_Week)* or *[Focus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focus_\(Ukrainian_magazine\))*, and television and radio are largely based there, although [Lviv](49968.md) is also a significant national media centre. The National News Agency of Ukraine, [Ukrinform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrinform) was founded here in 1918. [BBC Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Ukrainian) started its broadcasts in 1992.[^cite_note-430] As of 2022 75% of the population use the internet, and social media is widely used by government and people.[^cite_note-431]&#x20;

On 10 March 2024, creators of a documentary film *[20 Days in Mariupol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_Days_in_Mariupol)* were awarded with the [Oscar](324.md) in the category "Best Documentary Feature Film", the first Oscar in Ukraine's history.[^cite_note-432]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Ukrainian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_cuisine)



[Borscht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borscht) with [smetana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smetana_\(dairy_product\)) ([sour cream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sour_cream))

Ukrainian cuisine has been formed by the nation's tumultuous history, geography, culture and social customs. [Chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken) is the most consumed type of [protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein), accounting for about half of the meat intake. It is followed by [pork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pork) and [beef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beef).[^cite_note-433]<sup>: 12 </sup> Vegetables such as [potatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato), [cabbages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabbage), [mushrooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mushroom) and [beetroots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beetroot) are widely consumed.[^cite_note-434] [Pickled vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickling) are considered a delicacy.[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436] [Salo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salo_\(food\)), which is [cured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curing_\(food_preservation\)) pork fat, is considered the national delicacy.[^cite_note-437] Widely used [herbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herb) include [dill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dill), [parsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsley), [basil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil), [coriander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coriander) and [chives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chives).[^cite_note-hercules-438]&#x20;

Ukraine is often called the "[Breadbasket of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breadbasket#Europe)", and its plentiful [grain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain) and [cereal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cereal) resources such as [rye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rye) and [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat) play an important part in its cuisine; essential in making various kinds of bread.[^cite_note-bbccuisine-439][^cite_note-440] [Chernozem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernozem), the country's black-coloured highly fertile soil, produces some of the world's most flavourful crops.[^cite_note-441]&#x20;

Popular traditional dishes *_[varenyky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varenyky)_* ([dumplings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierogi#Ukraine)), *[nalysnyky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nalysnyky)* ([crêpes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cr%C3%AApe)), *[kapusnyak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabbage_soup)* (cabbage [soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soup)), [borscht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borscht) ([sour soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sour_soups)) and *[holubtsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Go%C5%82%C4%85bki)* ([cabbage rolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabbage_roll)).[^cite_note-bbccuisine-439] Among traditional baked goods are decorated [korovai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korovai) and [paska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paska_\(bread\)) ([easter bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_bread)).[^cite_note-442] Ukrainian specialties also include [Chicken Kiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_Kiev)[^cite_note-hercules-438] and [Kyiv cake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyiv_cake). Popular drinks include *uzvar* ([kompot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kompot) made of dried fruits),[^cite_note-hercules-438][^cite_note-hill-443] *[ryazhanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryazhenka)*,[^cite_note-444] and *_[horilka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horilka)_*.[^cite_note-hercules-438][^cite_note-hill-443] [Liquor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquor) (spirits) are the most consumed type of [alcoholic beverage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholic_beverage).[^cite_note-pmid-445] Alcohol consumption has seen a stark decrease, though by per capita, it remains [among the highest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_alcohol_consumption_per_capita).[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-pmid-445]&#x20;

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Ukraine)

Ukraine greatly benefited from the Soviet emphasis on [physical education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_education). These policies left Ukraine with hundreds of stadia, swimming pools, gymnasia and many other athletic facilities.[^cite_note-447] The most popular sport is [football](10568.md). The top professional league is the [Vyscha Liha](2322171.md) ("premier league").&#x20;

Many Ukrainians also played for the [Soviet national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_national_football_team), most notably [Ballon d'Or](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballon_d%27Or) winners [Ihor Belanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ihor_Belanov) and [Oleh Blokhin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleh_Blokhin). This award was only presented to one Ukrainian after the dissolution of the Soviet Union, [Andriy Shevchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andriy_Shevchenko). The national team made its debut in the [2006 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_FIFA_World_Cup), and reached the quarterfinals before losing to eventual champions, [Italy](362466.md).&#x20;

Ukrainian [boxers](4243.md) are amongst the best in the world.[^cite_note-448] Since becoming the undisputed cruiserweight champion in 2018, [Oleksandr Usyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleksandr_Usyk) has also gone on to win the unified WBA (Super), IBF, WBO and IBO heavyweight titles. This feat made him one of only three boxers to have unified the cruiserweight world titles and become a world heavyweight champion.[^cite_note-449] The brothers [Vitali](555483.md) and [Wladimir Klitschko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wladimir_Klitschko) are former [heavyweight](591011.md) world champions who held multiple world titles throughout their careers. Also hailing from Ukraine is [Vasyl Lomachenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasyl_Lomachenko), a [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics) and [2012 Olympic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Olympic_games) gold medalist. He is the [unified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_champion) [lightweight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lightweight) world champion who ties the record for winning a world title in the fewest professional fights; three.[^cite_note-450]&#x20;

[Sergey Bubka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Bubka) held the record in the [Pole vault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pole_vault) from 1993 to 2014; with great strength, speed and gymnastic abilities, he was voted the world's best athlete on several occasions.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452]&#x20;

[Basketball](3921.md) has gained popularity in Ukraine. In 2011, Ukraine was granted a right to organise [EuroBasket 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_2015). Two years later the [Ukraine national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_national_basketball_team) finished sixth in [EuroBasket 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_2013) and qualified to [FIBA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup) for the first time in its history. [Euroleague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroleague) participant [Budivelnyk Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BC_Budivelnyk) is the strongest professional basketball club in Ukraine.&#x20;

[Chess](5134.md) is a popular sport in Ukraine. [Ruslan Ponomariov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruslan_Ponomariov) is the former world champion. There are about 85 [Grandmasters](12304.md) and 198 [International Masters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Master_\(chess\)) in Ukraine. [Rugby league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_league) is played throughout Ukraine.[^cite_note-453]&#x20;
